Rabbi Yitzchak said: "Why are you in a hurry for him to carry out the ruling? This is my way: whoever claims a lie before me, one of his descendants will die. Perhaps he will regret it, and admit it, and live." And so it was: one of his descendants died, and he regretted it and admitted the truth.

**Summary:** A man who had to swear an oath in a lawsuit before Rabbi Yitzchak agreed to swear, and the other side, who knew he was lying, demanded that he swear at once. Rabbi Yitzchak said that for whoever lies before him one of his descendants dies, and so he was waiting; perhaps he would regret it. And so it was: one of his descendants died, and the man admitted the truth.

## 38 — The Oxen, and the Stolen Bridle

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Rabbi Yonah of Kaminka, the brother of Rabbi Baruch, sent oxen to be sold in the villages, at a time when there was a war with the Greeks. They heard that the Greeks had robbed Rabbi Yonah's oxen. Rabbi Baruch too had sent oxen on the road, and he was afraid that his oxen would be robbed as well. They sent Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka to the Baal Shem Tov, and found him washing his hands for the meal. The Baal Shem Tov told Rabbi Yosef to wash his hands too, and after the blessing over the bread he said: "Why did they not speak with me when they set out? I would have guarded them." Afterward the Baal Shem Tov opened the Zohar, looked into it for one moment, and said: "I see Rabbi Baruch's oxen, that they are safe, and they were not robbed."

Rabbi Yosef asked the Baal Shem Tov: "And that is written in the Zohar?" He answered him: "This is what our Sages said on the verse 'And God saw the light, that it was good': that it was good to hide it away. For by the light of the six days of creation one could look from one end of the world to the other. And where did the Holy One, blessed be He, hide it? In the Torah. And what they said, 'for the righteous,' means: for the righteous who will come into the world in the future. And everyone who merits to find the hidden light in the Torah looks by it from one end of the world to the other. And you think I saw only the oxen? In that same seeing I also saw something that happened in the city of Amsterdam." And he told him the story, and I did not hear it.

A story: the Baal Shem Tov wanted to set out on a journey, and since it was the time for the blessing of the new moon, he waited until night. When he went to bless the new moon, one of the companions went with him, and wanted to look back, and the Baal Shem Tov did not let him look, for he said: "Someone is stealing the horses' bridle." (The horses were harnessed to the wagon, so as to leave right after the blessing of the new moon.) "And he is stealing because he has no money for the Sabbath expenses." After the blessing of the new moon the attendant began to shout: "Who stole the bridle?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Do not shout. Go to such-and-such a man, take eighteen groschen, and redeem the bridle, for the thief pawned it there. And do not make the matter public."

**Summary:** When Rabbi Baruch was afraid that robbers would take his oxen, the Baal Shem Tov looked for a moment into the Zohar and said they were safe, and explained that the light by which one sees from one end of the world to the other is hidden in the Torah. Another time, on the way to bless the new moon, the Baal Shem Tov knew that a poor man was stealing the horses' bridle so as to have money for the Sabbath. He did not let his companion look, and afterward sent the attendant to quietly redeem the bridle from the man with whom it was pawned.

## 39 — The Drunken Shochet

It happened in the country: there was a shochet who was very frivolous and a drunkard. Once the Rabbi of Polonne called a certain man, after the morning prayer, into a room, and said to him: "I saw the Baal Shem Tov, and he went over the matter of the shochet before me several times. I cannot explain how the thing was, for he also went over the matter of the gentile butchers before me. Even so, the main thing was his strictness about the shochet, and he told me to keep an eye on him, and to catch him at something." And so it was. The rabbi was very careful not to harm a person's livelihood, and so the shochet had kept his post all this time. "And since I caught him, the rabbi ordered a rabbinical court to be convened, and testimony to be taken from the butcher, and if by Torah law it was so, they were to remove him from his post." The butcher testified about the matter, and they removed the shochet.

**Summary:** In a certain town there was a frivolous, drunken shochet, and the Rabbi of Polonne, who was very careful not to harm a person's livelihood, left him in his post. After the Baal Shem Tov appeared to the rabbi and went over the matter of the shochet before him several times, the rabbi ordered that an eye be kept on him. When they caught him, a rabbinical court sat, the butcher testified, and the shochet was removed from his post.

## 40 — Rabbi Avraham, the Maggid's Son

A story: when Rabbi Avraham, the son of the great Maggid, was taken on as preacher in the town of Fastov, the matchmaker was the famous pious Rabbi Menachem Nachum of Chernobyl. I heard from Rabbi Yoel of Pratahor, who spent the festival of Passover with Rabbi Nachum in the town of Pohrebishtch: the day after Yom Kippur a messenger came to Rabbi Nachum from Rabbi Avraham, for Rabbi Avraham had grown weak on Yom Kippur, and at the time of Ne'ilah he had stopped speaking. They asked him whether to send for Rabbi Nachum, and he nodded his head to mean "yes." They sent for him, and meanwhile Rabbi Avraham passed away. Rabbi Nachum went to the mikveh, for he said: "I will pray for him in my own place." Before the festival (Sukkot) they heard that Rabbi Avraham had passed away, but they did not tell Rabbi Nachum. On the intermediate days of the festival a man revealed it to Rabbi Nachum in all innocence, and Rabbi Nachum began to beat his head against the wall, and wept a great weeping for about two hours. They could not quiet him, until they took Rabbi Nachum in their arms, carried him to the sukkah, and said: "Rebbe, remember that today is a festival!" And then his weeping calmed down.

He began to speak in praise of Rabbi Avraham, and said: "Once I was in the town of Anipoli, and I asked him whether he sees his father (the Maggid, who had already passed away). He said: 'Yes.' I asked: 'In a dream or awake?' He answered: 'He comes to me in a dream and speaks with me, and afterward I wake from my sleep, and he speaks with me awake.'" Rabbi Avraham told Rabbi Nachum that there was a rich man there who had caused him grief several times. (Rabbi Yoel did not want to make this rich man's name public.)

"I complained to my father," said Rabbi Avraham, "and my father said to me: 'I will send for him.' I asked: 'Where will you call him to?' He said to me: 'To your study house.' And he sent for him my servant, who is called Rabbi Filet." Rabbi Filet was at that time in the region of Polesia, about sixty parsahs from Anipoli. He came to the rich man's house, knocked at the door and said: "Open for me!" The rich man recognized him by his voice, and was surprised, and said in his heart: "But he is not at home." And at once he explained it to himself: "Perhaps he came home toward evening." He greeted him with great joy, and they asked each other how they were. Afterward Rabbi Filet said: "There is no time to talk at length, for the rabbi is calling you." The rich man said to him: "What are you talking about? Our master has already died and gone to his eternal world!" Rabbi Filet said to him: "Even so, he is calling you." And the rich man went with him.

When the rich man came to Rabbi Avraham's study house, he saw the Maggid sitting at the head of the table, dressed in clothes as he had been in his lifetime. The Maggid said to him: "You think I am not here, and you do whatever your heart desires. So I am showing you that I am sitting here." The rich man began to justify himself and make excuses, but the Maggid did not want to listen, and said to him: "Go away from me! Go away from me!" And he rebuked him: "Filet, take him home!" And Filet took him home. When they reached his house, Rabbi Filet said to him: "Go into your house in peace, for I have to go back." When the rich man went into his house, he fainted, and there was a great outcry, until his neighbors gathered and revived him. They asked him why he had fainted, and he told them the story. They said to him: "Let us go to Rabbi Avraham." They all went to Rabbi Avraham, and when he saw them, he laughed and said: "It seems my father called him." They said to him: "How do you know?" He answered: "Because my father told me he would call him."

**Summary:** Rabbi Avraham, the son of the Maggid of Mezritch, passed away after Yom Kippur, and Rabbi Nachum of Chernobyl wept over him greatly and spoke in his praise. Rabbi Avraham had told him that his father the Maggid, after his passing, would come to him and speak with him. Once, when a rich man caused Rabbi Avraham grief, the Maggid sent the servant Rabbi Filet, who was very far away, to bring the rich man at night to the study house, and there the rich man saw the Maggid sitting at the head of the table, and the Maggid rebuked him and sent him home.

## 41 — Rabbi Avraham as Sandek

Rabbi Nachum also told: Rabbi Avraham lived in the town of Fastov closed off and secluded, and the people of the town did not merit to see his face. Rabbi Nachum praised him before the people of the town, and they longed to see him. Once there was a circumcision. They gave Rabbi Nachum all the honors of the circumcision, and to Rabbi Avraham they gave the honor of being sandek. After the prayer they waited for him a long time, for he drew out his prayer. A great crowd gathered in the synagogue to see him. And when they said "Rabbi Avraham is coming to the synagogue," fear fell on them, and no more than about twenty men remained in the synagogue.

When he came to the synagogue (and his walking was a run), the rabbi of the community understood that he was going to his place, and moved aside into the corner. Rabbi Avraham came and sat in the rabbi's place, and rested his head on his hand, on the lectern. They brought the baby to the bimah, and Rabbi Avraham was still leaning on his hand. They said to the shammes: "Go and tell him that they have brought the baby," and the shammes would not go, out of great fear. They told others to go and tell him, and no one wanted to go. Meanwhile Rabbi Avraham himself came to the bimah.

Rabbi Nachum said: "I was so afraid when he came that the knife fell from my hand, and I caught the knife by the edge of the blade. And to this day I do not remember whether I said the blessings of the circumcision, from so much fear. I let him say the blessings over the *metzitzah*, and he said them." When Rabbi Nachum came home, he sat silent and wondering. His attendant brought him coffee to drink twice, and he did not want it. The attendant pressed him and begged him to drink it after all, and Rabbi Nachum answered him: "Fool! How can one drink coffee, when one hears that a man serves God with such service?"

**Summary:** Rabbi Avraham, the Maggid's son, lived in Fastov in seclusion, and the people of the town hardly ever saw him. When he was sandek at a circumcision, everyone was afraid of him, and Rabbi Nachum of Chernobyl, who was the mohel, was so afraid that the knife fell from his hand. Afterward he could not even drink coffee, for he said that one cannot drink when one hears that a man serves God with such service.

## 42 — The Knife That Fell

(And in the name of our master, the Rebbe, we heard that Rabbi Menachem of Chernobyl was the sandek, and Rabbi Avraham was the mohel. And when Rabbi Avraham said the blessing of the circumcision with great awe, the knife fell from his hand, from fear and trembling before the greatness of God. Afterward Rabbi Menachem asked him: "What is the nature of this great awe?" He answered him: "It amazes me how one can ask such a question.")

**Summary:** According to another version of the story, Rabbi Avraham was the mohel, and the knife fell from his hand from great awe as he said the blessing. When Rabbi Menachem of Chernobyl asked him what this awe was, he answered that he wondered how one could ask such a question.

## 43 — The Maggid's Daughter-in-Law

I heard from Rabbi Menachem, who heard it from the daughter-in-law of the great Maggid, the wife of Rabbi Avraham: when the Maggid's holy son, Rabbi Avraham, became a widower, the Maggid called two of the leading men of the town and said to them: "I ask you to travel to the town of Kremenets, and arrange a match for my son with the daughter of Rabbi Feivel the rabbi, the author of the book *Mishnat Chachamim*, and bring her here." At once they did as he commanded. They prepared a good wagon and horses for themselves, and also fine clothes, to do honor to the rabbi, and set out, until they came to Kremenets and stopped at an inn. When they had rested from the journey, right after the prayer, they went to Rabbi Feivel's house.

Rabbi Feivel's custom was not to engage in any business of making a living in the world, but only in Torah, all day and all night. Only his wife, the mistress of the house, saw to their livelihood. When they came to his house, his wife came back from the synagogue. She greeted them and said: "My brothers, where have you come from?" They said: "From Mezritch." She said: "What are you doing here? Some business, or something else?" And they told her that our great master had sent them to speak about her daughter, that she should marry his son, who had become a widower not long ago. The thing seemed laughable to her, for they did not know the rabbi, and had not heard of him. And besides, the girl was only twelve years old, and it had never entered their minds to look for matches for their daughter yet. After much talking, the words entered her ears. She said: "Thank God, my husband is at home. What do I care? Let him do as he wishes." They sent for him from the study house, and he came home. They put the matter before Rabbi Feivel, until the thing seemed right to him, and at once he ordered the scribe to come and write the engagement contract, in a good hour.

When they began to write the engagement contract, the messengers said: "Write whatever you like about the dowry. Only on the matter of the time of the wedding, it cannot possibly be put off." The bride's parents argued: "But our Sages said that a girl is given twelve months. And especially since we had not yet thought of marrying her off soon, and she has no clothes and other things ready." The messengers answered: "About that we are not particular. But it is impossible to put off the time of the wedding. She must travel with us at once." And since the thing had come from God, they agreed to this too. It was settled that Rabbi Feivel, so as not to interrupt his study, would stay at home, and the bride would travel with her mother to the wedding. They hired a good wagon and horses for them too, and they traveled with the messengers.

All the way the mother wondered at herself: what had happened to them, that they had let themselves be talked into this? Afterward they came to the town of Rovno, and all the people of the town came, from small to great, men, women and children, to welcome them and greet the bride of the great rabbi. And then her mind was set a little at rest. Afterward, when they reached Mezritch, men and children went out to meet them outside the town, until they had to drive slowly because of the great crowd that had come out to meet them. When they entered the town, all the women came out to meet them and greet them, until they came opposite the rabbi's house. The Maggid and his son came out to welcome them, and greeted them. Afterward they drove to their lodging. The wedding went on for the seven days of feasting with great joy, and words of Torah were said in abundance. After the wedding the bride's mother went home with great joy, for she had never seen or heard anything like it.

The Maggid drew his daughter-in-law very close. One day the daughter-in-law dreamed that she went into great halls, and there sat a Sanhedrin of honored elders, and they wanted to take her husband from her. She cried out before them, and made several claims before the court. She woke up, and it was a dream. But she did not tell anyone, and although she lived in the Maggid's house, she did not tell him. On the second night she dreamed the same dream again, and said nothing. And on the third night she dreamed it again, and pleaded much before them, until in the end they said to her: "See, because your claims are very just, we are giving him to you as a gift for twelve years." The next day, when she came to the Maggid in the morning, the Maggid praised her highly, and blessed her for her just claims, which had lengthened her husband's time by twelve years. And so it was.

After some years the Maggid went up to Heaven and passed away. Even so, in every trouble of theirs, or when they needed something, the Maggid would appear to her in a dream, and tell her what to do. Once, on Thursday night, he came to her in a dream and said: "Tell your husband to exchange his room for yours, or at least to put his books in your room." In the morning she told her husband, and he paid no attention to it, and said: "Why did he not tell me, and told you?" And because of our many sins his room burned down with the books.

Afterward Rabbi Avraham traveled about the region, and when he came to the town of Fastov, they received him with great honor, took him as their rabbi, and set a salary for him. And they sent a messenger to his wife, so that she too would come there for the Days of Awe. When they came to take her, the Maggid came to her in a dream and said to her: "Do not travel to Fastov in these wagons." In the morning she refused to go, and there was a great uproar among the tzaddikim Rabbi Meshulam Zusya and Rabbi Yehudah Leib HaKohen, for they wanted her to go there, to fulfill the will of the tzaddik. But she paid no attention to them. Not many days passed, and because of our many sins "the ark of God was hidden away" (the tzaddik passed away) there, in Fastov, and they sent a messenger to Anipoli. The tzaddikim were very ashamed before her, that the woman had seen better than they had. But they hid the matter from her, and revealed it only to her son, Rabbi Shalom the rabbi, who was small, so that he would say Kaddish, without revealing it to his mother.

But she, in her great wisdom, wondered at the eagerness of her son, who got up early in the morning to go to the synagogue, which he was not used to doing. When she went to the synagogue, she leaned against the wall of the study house, to hear whether her son was saying the mourner's Kaddish. She could not make it out exactly, until after the prayer she coaxed her son with words, and said: "Why are you hiding it from me? I heard from behind the wall that you said the mourner's Kaddish." Until he had to reveal the truth, and she mourned for her husband. After the seven days of mourning, she traveled to Fastov to receive what her husband had left. When she arrived there, they received her with great honor. In her honor they made a meal at her lodging that Sabbath, and all our fellow Hasidim ate there in her honor, to comfort her a little, but she would not be comforted.

At the third Sabbath meal almost all the people of the town gathered there, and sang the Sabbath songs as is the custom. And she, in the bitterness of her heart, sat sadly on the bed with the wife of the master of the house. She dozed off, and dreamed: great halls, beautiful and splendid. She opened a door, and her husband, Rabbi Avraham, came into the house shining like the sun at noon, very happy. After him came in several honored elders. He said to them: "Gentlemen, sit down." And they sat around the table. He said to them: "My wife has complaints against me about several things in which I acted with too much separation from the world, and she is right. And I ask her forgiveness before you." She said: "You are forgiven with a whole heart." And he also said before them: "The Torah has permitted her to marry another husband, especially since she is young, twenty-four years old, and I cannot prevent her. But if she agrees not to marry another, then all her needs are on me. And her sons, surely even before she gets home, each one of them will have a good position." And she woke up. And at once she no longer had an angry face, for she was comforted by his words.

When she came home, matches came at once for her sons and her daughters. God sent blessing on the work of her hands, and she made an honorable living from the shop she had, so that she lacked nothing. And in time of need her father-in-law, the Maggid, would appear to her, to tell her what to do.

After a long time the wife of the great rabbi Rabbi Menachem of Chernobyl died, and he wanted to take this woman as his wife. He took counsel with her son, Rabbi Shalom the rabbi, who was his son-in-law (his daughter's husband), and it seemed good to him too, for she was still young. Rabbi Menachem sent Rabbi Shalom to speak with his mother. On the way Rabbi Shalom dreamed: great halls, beautiful and splendid, and his father, the holy rabbi, was standing in the doorway of the hall, with both his hands on the roof, and crying out in a loud voice: "Who is he, and where is he, whose heart has dared to enter my hall?" He woke up, and understood how far the matter reached, and went back home in peace.

**Summary:** The Maggid of Mezritch sent messengers to Kremenets to arrange a match for his widowed son, Rabbi Avraham, with a girl of twelve, and insisted that she come at once, and all the towns on the way came out to receive her with honor. The bride dreamed that a court in Heaven wanted to take her husband, and because of her claims they gave him twelve more years, and even after the Maggid's death he appeared to her in dreams and warned her. After her husband's passing she saw him in a dream asking her forgiveness and promising to provide for her and her children if she did not remarry, and when Rabbi Menachem of Chernobyl wanted to marry her, her son dreamed that his father was crying out: "Who dared to enter my hall?"

## 44 — Rabbi Aaron of Karlin and the Tefillin

The great Maggid, Rabbi Dov of Mezritch, once commanded his disciple, Rabbi Aaron of Karlin, to travel home by way of a certain community. Rabbi Aaron did as he commanded. When he came to that town, he searched his bag, and saw that he had forgotten the bag with his tallit and tefillin. And Rabbi Aaron had never prayed in a borrowed tallit and tefillin. He sent to a shop to buy a tallit and to put tzitzit on it, and he himself went to the scribe to buy tefillin. When the scribe put the tefillin into his hand, Rabbi Aaron at once cried out: "Yesterday you sinned with a married woman, and today you sell tefillin? Wicked man, confess!" And the scribe confessed his sin. After he confessed, they made inquiries and investigations, and found many men and women who had all been defiled by sins, until more than a hundred bills of divorce were needed. In one matter Rabbi Aaron had a question, and he did not know what to do, and he had to go back to the great Maggid. <!-- MATURE: adultery across a town, more than a hundred divorces -->

When he came, the Maggid told him everything that had happened, and also the question about which he was in doubt, and what the law was in practice. Before Rabbi Aaron traveled to that town again, the Maggid said to him: "Let Rabbi Menachem Mendel, who knows how to speak Polish, go with you. And also take your white Sabbath clothes with you." And so they did. On the way Rabbi Menachem Mendel said: "Our journey is only to arrange the bills of divorce. If so, why is it necessary to speak Polish, and white clothes too? It can only be that our master sees with the eye of the mind that the wicked have regretted confessing, and have informed on us to the lord of the town." And so it was. When they reached the town, the lord posted guards to bring them to him at once, and he wanted to do them harm. The wicked hid with the lord in another room.

When they came to the lord, dressed in white clothes, they found favor in his eyes. He received them with great honor, and told them to sit beside him. In the course of the conversation the lord asked them: "Why have these rabbis come here?" Rabbi Menachem Mendel said: "Rabbi Aaron is a man of God, and he saw that there are great accusations against your town, that literally destruction has been decreed on it, because the people of the town are evil and sin greatly against God, especially in matters of adultery. Many women must be divorced, and we have come to you, for you are the lord of the town, and you must see to their good." As they spoke to him, the lord remembered the words of the wicked. He ordered them to come out and speak with the rabbis. At once Rabbi Aaron thundered at them with a voice of noise and anger, and they had to confess all the sins before the lord. The lord ordered them to carry out the divorces, and gave the rabbis great authority.

**Summary:** The Maggid of Mezritch sent Rabbi Aaron of Karlin to a certain town, and there, when he bought tefillin, he sensed at once that the scribe had sinned with a married woman, and the scribe confessed. So it came out that many in the town had sinned, and more than a hundred bills of divorce were needed. When Rabbi Aaron went back to the town, the Maggid sent with him Rabbi Menachem Mendel, who knew Polish, and white clothes, because the sinners had informed on them to the lord. The lord was impressed by the rabbis, the sinners confessed before him, and the lord ordered the bills of divorce to be arranged.

## 45 — The Messengers to the Duke

In a certain community the people of the town suffered great torment and pressure from their commissar, until they truly could not bear it. They decided that they had no other way but to send messengers to the duke, who was about eighty parsahs away from them. They chose two important men: one old and respected, and the other young. The people of the community warned them to hurry on their journey, for the time of the *kontrakt* (when the contracts were signed) was near, and if they were even one day late, damage would be done that they could not repair.

Their way passed through Mezritch. They reached the inn on Sunday morning, and prayed there. Meanwhile the innkeeper asked them what their business was and where they were going, and they told him everything. The innkeeper said: "My advice is that you go to our great master, and ask his holy mouth what to do." The old man was a little unwilling, but the young man's heart was drawn to the advice, and he persuaded the old man to go to the rabbi, and so they did. When they came to the rabbi, they found a large crowd there wanting to go in, and they waited several hours, until they found his attendant, and told him that they wanted to go in to the rabbi. The attendant went to the rabbi, and came back and said to them in the rabbi's name: "Stay here tonight, and tomorrow he will answer you." The delay was very hard for the old man, for they were in a great hurry, and the time was very short. Even so, they had to stay there overnight.

The next day, after the prayer, they came to the rabbi's house again, and again waited a long time, until their turn came. The attendant went to the rabbi again, and came back and said to them in the rabbi's name: "Stay here one more night." This was very hard for them, and they deeply regretted having gone to him. Even so, they had to stay until Tuesday. On Tuesday an answer came to them from the rabbi: that they should not travel by the usual road, but by another road, which was truly roundabout. The old man was very angry with the young man, that they had stayed until now for nothing. Even so, they listened to his words, and traveled by the road the rabbi had commanded.

As they traveled, bitter about the delay, the sky was covered with clouds, and a pouring rain came down on them. In the middle of it they lost their way, and they were in very great distress. So they traveled, until they came to a village. They were frozen through from the rain and the wind, and stopped there for the night. They gave the horses to a gentile to graze them at night, and told him to bring the horses early in the morning. The next day they rose early, prayed, and finished their prayer, and still the horses had not been brought. They were in very great distress over the delay, for they were afraid the set time would pass. Meanwhile the gentile came, and said that the horses had been seized and taken to the nobleman's court, because they had been found doing damage. They asked the innkeeper to go to the nobleman and ask for the horses. The innkeeper said that he would certainly not go to the nobleman, for besides the fact that he would surely not give back the horses, he would even beat him with cruel blows, for that was always his way. They wanted to pay the innkeeper for his trouble in going there, and he would not do it in any way, for his body was dearer to him than his money. And they had to go to the nobleman themselves, in the bitterness of their hearts.

When they reached the court, they saw a servant, and it seemed to them that he was a servant of their own duke. And when the servant saw them, he too recognized that they were from that community, for he had been there several times with the duke. They asked the servant: "What are you doing here?" He answered: "I am here with the duke." They were filled with joy, and went with him to the duke. When the duke saw them, he was very glad to see them, and asked them where they were going. They told him everything that had happened, and obtained from him everything they asked for, and he gave them letters. They also said before him: "Our horses have been seized by the nobleman." The duke sent to the nobleman to give them back their horses at once. And they traveled on with great joy, and arrived several days before the set time. And they saw with their own eyes that it had been the word of God from beginning to end.

**Summary:** Two messengers who were traveling urgently to the duke, to save their community from a cruel commissar, stopped on the way at the Maggid of Mezritch, and he held them up for two days and sent them by a roundabout road. They lost their way in the rain, and their horses were seized at the court of a cruel nobleman, but when they went to ask for them, they found the duke himself there. The duke gave them everything they asked for, gave them back their horses, and they arrived before the set time.

## 46 — The Author of *Chesed LeAvraham*

What they heard from the holy Rabbi Zusya of Anipoli, who heard it from his brother, Rabbi Elimelech the rabbi: once he went up on high, and met the rabbi who wrote the book *Chesed LeAvraham*. And the author asked him: "Rebbe, I have written, with God's help, ten holy works, and they are very important in the Garden of Eden. Tell me: why are all the worlds in an uproar from your movements down below? Tell me what you are doing, for we cannot understand how deep they are, how far these things reach."

**Summary:** Rabbi Elimelech went up to Heaven and met the author of *Chesed LeAvraham*, who wrote ten holy books. The author asked him why all the worlds are in an uproar from the movements of the Hasidim down below, for in Heaven they do not understand how deep their service is.

## 47 — Rabbi Shmelke and Rabbi Pinchas

The holy Rabbi Shmelke and his brother, Rabbi Pinchas the rabbi, were both asked at the same time to serve as rabbis: in Frankfurt and in Nikolsburg. Since Frankfurt was a larger and more honored community than Nikolsburg, the people of the communities did not decide which of them would sit on the rabbi's seat in Frankfurt and which in Nikolsburg, and said that they themselves would decide between them. Although Rabbi Shmelke was older than his brother, out of his great humility he said that his brother, Rabbi Pinchas, was more fit for the post in Frankfurt. And his brother gave the greatness to his brother, who was older than he. They both agreed that when they took leave of our great master, the Maggid of Mezritch, they would ask his holy mouth, and as he decreed, so they would do. They also agreed to ask him about a passage of the Zohar that they had not been able to understand.

When they came to the rabbi's house, the brothers began to argue with each other over who would go in first, for each one told his brother that he was greater than he. At once the Maggid said this: "The rabbi of Nikolsburg will come in first, for he is the elder of the brothers." When they came in, the Maggid told them a story about a nobleman, and about his ways with his servants and his palaces. When he finished the story, he told them to go in peace. They did not ask him about the passage of the Zohar. When they came to their lodging, they sat down to think about the matter, for surely he had not told them the story for nothing. And they saw with open eyes that it was a parable, and from it they understood the passage of the Zohar thoroughly.

**Summary:** The brothers Rabbi Shmelke and Rabbi Pinchas were chosen as rabbis in Frankfurt and in Nikolsburg, and each one wanted to give his brother the more important city. When they came to the Maggid of Mezritch, he said at once that the rabbi of Nikolsburg would come in first, for he was the elder, and told them a story about a nobleman. Only at their lodging did they understand that the story was a parable, and that in it was the explanation of the passage of the Zohar that they had not understood and had not asked about.

## 48 — The Messengers Who Came to Test the Baal Shem Tov

I heard from the rabbi of our community, Rabbi Gedaliah, that the pious Rabbi Yechiel, head of the rabbinical court of Kovel, sent a learned and wise man "to examine the jug" of the Baal Shem Tov (to test him). The man fell ill on the way, and reached Mezhibuzh, and lay on the stove at the inn with a high fever. The Baal Shem Tov sent his brother-in-law, Rabbi Gershon, to him. Rabbi Gershon sat by him on the stove, and spoke words of Torah before him, and afterward said to him: "Come with me to the afternoon prayer." On the way the man felt that he was well, and thought this was the Baal Shem Tov. He asked him: "Are you the Baal Shem Tov?" Rabbi Gershon answered him: "Oh, oh! I, the Rebbe? If only I had a World to Come like what our master earns in the smoke of one pipe!" And Rabbi Yechiel drew close to the Baal Shem Tov.

I heard from my brother-in-law, Rabbi Mordechai, who heard it from Rabbi Yechiel of Kovel: once a man came from Lithuania, whom the people of his country had sent to test the Baal Shem Tov. He stayed with Rabbi Yechiel, and ate at the Baal Shem Tov's. When he heard the Baal Shem Tov's prayer on Friday evening, it pleased him very much, and after the prayer he followed the Baal Shem Tov to his house. When the Baal Shem Tov came home, he began to shout at his servant, and was angry with him that he should go quickly to the horses, for a horse was choking. The servant pleaded with him and said: "Sir, I am going at once." And the Baal Shem Tov again was angry with him, and wanted to beat him. The servant went and saved the horse. When the guest saw the Baal Shem Tov's anger, he was greatly amazed, and said in his heart: "After such a prayer, one horse matters so much to him that he gets angry at the servant? And on the Sabbath at that?" In the morning, when he heard his prayer, it pleased him, and even so this question stayed stuck in his heart.

After the Sabbath, when he wanted to go home, he revealed the matter to Rabbi Yechiel, and went on his way. Rabbi Yechiel told these things before the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov grew angry and said: "What business do they have examining my jug? I will tell you: a Jew was traveling on the road, and the Sabbath came in on him, and he could not reach a settlement for the Sabbath. He decided to turn off the road into a field, to spend the Sabbath there. A robber came, and began to beat him, and wanted to kill him. This Jew was very coarse, and I could not strengthen him except by means of an animal. So I cast terror and fear on my servant, and the more fear I cast on my servant, the more fear fell on the robbers, until they left him alive. And you will see that they will soon bring him into town." And so it was.

**Summary:** Rabbi Yechiel of Kovel sent a scholar to test the Baal Shem Tov, and the scholar fell ill on the way, and was healed when Rabbi Gershon sat by him and said Torah, and he thought that Rabbi Gershon was the Baal Shem Tov, but Rabbi Gershon said that he came nowhere near his master's greatness. A guest from Lithuania, who had also come to test the Baal Shem Tov, was amazed that after an exalted prayer the Baal Shem Tov grew angry at a servant over a horse on the Sabbath. The Baal Shem Tov explained that it was to save a Jew whom a robber had attacked in a field: the fear he cast on the servant fell on the robbers, and the Jew was saved.

## 49 — The Baal Shem Tov and Rabbi Saadia Gaon

I heard from my father-in-law that the Baal Shem Tov said of himself that he was a reincarnation of Rabbi Saadia Gaon. I told this before the rabbi of our community, and the rabbi said that he knew that the Baal Shem Tov studied the books of Rabbi Saadia Gaon diligently. Once people asked him to preach before them after the prayer, and he preached. In the middle of the sermon he swayed and trembled, as at prayer, and said in the middle of the sermon: "Master of the world, it is revealed and known before You that I do not say this for my own honor (but for the honor of my father's house and my mother's house): I have known much and been able to do much, and there is no man to whom I can reveal it."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov said of himself that he was a reincarnation of Rabbi Saadia Gaon, and indeed he studied his books a great deal. Once, in the middle of a sermon, he trembled as at prayer and said before God that he knew and could do much, and there was no one to whom he could reveal it.

## 50 — The Harmful Spirits in the Synagogue, and the Rabbi's Wife in Childbirth

I heard from the rabbi of our community, and also from the Rabbi of Polonne, that in the town of Izbarazh two "jesters" (harmful spirits) were seen in the women's synagogue. The women were afraid, until they had to leave the synagogue. Rabbi Chaim, the preacher of our community, went and sat in the synagogue and studied there, and drove the jesters out of it. They went, and harmed two of his children. Rabbi Chaim sent for the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov spent the night in Rabbi Chaim's house of seclusion with Rabbi Tzvi the scribe. The Baal Shem Tov ordered that the children be brought to him, to the place where he was staying, and so they did.

When they lay down to sleep, the Baal Shem Tov's bed was at the head of the table, and the scribe lay on the other side, by the Baal Shem Tov's head. Even before they fell asleep, the two harmful spirits came into the house, stood by the door, and mocked the Baal Shem Tov, the way he sings "Lecha Dodi." The Baal Shem Tov got up, sat on his bed, and said to the scribe: "Did you see?" The scribe saw it too, and pushed his head under the Baal Shem Tov's pillow, and said: "Leave me alone!" When they finished speaking, the harmful spirits went toward the children. The Baal Shem Tov jumped out of bed, rebuked them and said to them: "Where are you going?" And they were not afraid, and said: "What do you care?" And they began again to mock and to sing "Lecha Dodi" to the Baal Shem Tov's tune. The Baal Shem Tov did what he did, and the two harmful spirits fell to the ground, and could not get up.

They began to plead with him, and he said: "You will see at once that the children will get well." One of them said: "What has already happened cannot be repaired, for we harmed the inner organs. And now we came to finish them off completely. But it is their good luck that your honor is here." The Baal Shem Tov asked them: "How, and what, and why did you come to the synagogue?" They answered: "The cantor prayed in the synagogue, and he had a singer, called a 'bass,' who was a great adulterer. When he sang, his intention was to please the women. He had impure thoughts, and the women too had impure thoughts about him, and from these two kinds of thoughts we were created, two harmful spirits, male and female. And our life is in the synagogue." Then the Baal Shem Tov gave them a place by a well, in a place where there are no people. <!-- MATURE: a singer described as an adulterer; demons created from impure thoughts -->

The pious Rabbi Yosef of Zornitz, who was called Rabbi Yosef Melamed, was in our town, Linitz. I was not well at that time, and could not sit with him; I only greeted him. I did not hear it from him myself, but my friends heard it from him and told me. Money of other people was always deposited with Rabbi Yosef (money held in trust for others), and the money of the rabbi's wife was with him too. At that time coins were withdrawn from use, and there were quarrels because of it. Rabbi Yosef said: "Let us go to the rabbi, the Maggid, and he will make a compromise between us." They went to the Maggid, and he made a compromise between them. The rabbi's wife went home, and Rabbi Yosef stayed on with the Maggid. The Maggid said to Rabbi Yosef: "This rabbi's wife is already beyond hope."

The next day the rabbi's wife felt labor pains, and understood that her labor was hard. She sent for Rabbi Yosef, and said to him: "I ask your honor to forgive me. So go to the Maggid, so that he will make a redemption of the soul for me." (And she had heard what the Maggid said.) Rabbi Yosef said to her: "If you give me the key to the chest, so that I can take from it as much money as I want, I will go. And if not, I will not go." She gave him the key, and he took a hundred and sixty zlotys, and came to the Maggid. The Maggid said to him: "Why have you come? I already told you what her condition is." Rabbi Yosef said: "She gave me the key, and gave me permission over all the money she has." At once the Maggid sent for Rabbi Michel, Rabbi Leib HaKohen and Rabbi Yosef, to go to the mikveh. The Maggid could still walk on his own feet, but he could not go into the water. The three of them went, and the Maggid went with them too. The three went into the mikveh, and the Maggid stood above them.

They stayed under the water, and when they came up out of the water, the Maggid said: "You have not yet achieved it." And so they immersed several times. (Rabbi Yosef said that the Maggid wanted to teach him the mystical intentions, "but I prefer to go in my simplicity.") They stayed in the mikveh a very long time, and Rabbi Yosef was frozen from the cold, for he was old, and he said: "Master of the world, give me the rabbi's wife as a gift, and if not, I am in danger!" Afterward the Maggid ordered them to come out of the mikveh and go up, for "thank God, you have achieved that the rabbi's wife will live, and will bear three children." (And he said how many sons and how many daughters, and I do not remember.) Even before they went to the mikveh the Maggid had ordered a dish cooked with many spices, so that Rabbi Yosef would warm up after the mikveh. After he had eaten, the Maggid told him to hide, so that they would not find him, for there would be a great commotion. He also locked the door behind him, and did not let anyone in, until the rabbi's wife gave birth to the children, and stayed alive.

**Summary:** Two harmful spirits, created from the impure thoughts of a singer in the synagogue and of the women, frightened the women in the synagogue and harmed the children of the preacher Rabbi Chaim. The Baal Shem Tov threw them to the ground, they pleaded and revealed to him how they had been created, and he sent them to live by a well in a place where there are no people. In the second story, the Maggid of Mezritch said that a certain rabbi's wife was beyond hope, and Rabbi Yosef Melamed and his companions immersed again and again in the mikveh under his guidance, until the rabbi's wife bore three children and stayed alive.

## 51 — The House Next to the Church

I heard from Rabbi Meir of Anipoli that Rabbi Yosef Melamed lived in a certain town (I have forgotten the name of the town), and his house was next to a house of idol worship (a church). Once the Baal Shem Tov came to his town, and saw his house next to the house of idol worship, and said: "In this house lives a righteous man." When Rabbi Yosef came to welcome him, he asked him: "Why do you live next to a house of idol worship?" He answered him: "I am a very poor man. If I had money, I would move my house somewhere else." And not much time passed, and the house of idol worship burned down, and the lord of the town did not allow another to be built in that place.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw that Rabbi Yosef Melamed, out of great poverty, lived next to a church, and said that in this house lived a righteous man. A short time later the church burned down, and the lord of the town did not allow it to be rebuilt there.

## 52 — The Craftsman Who Sold Stockings

I also heard from Rabbi Meir: in that town there was a craftsman called Shipovel. He prayed in the synagogue regularly, summer and winter, and even when there were not ten in the synagogue, he prayed alone. Once the Baal Shem Tov was in that town. As he was smoking his pipe before the prayer, he looked out of the window, and saw the craftsman going to the synagogue, and was deeply shaken. The Baal Shem Tov said to his host: "Go out and see who that is walking, with a tallit and tefillin in his hand." The host went out, looked, and said: "A craftsman is going to the synagogue." The Baal Shem Tov said to call him to come to him. The host said to him: "I know he is odd, and he certainly will not want to come." And the Baal Shem Tov was silent.

After the prayer the Baal Shem Tov sent for the craftsman, to bring him four pairs of stockings. He came, and brought the stockings with him. The Baal Shem Tov asked him: "How much for a pair of stockings?" He said: "A zloty and a half." He said to him: "Perhaps one zloty a pair?" And the craftsman answered him nothing. The Baal Shem Tov ordered another man to bargain with him, and that man said to him: "Perhaps you will sell for less than a zloty and a half?" He said to him: "If I had wanted to sell for less, I would have said a lower price the first time." And the Baal Shem Tov paid him for the four pairs.

The Baal Shem Tov asked him: "What do you do?" He said: "I work at my craft." He asked him: "And how do you work?" He said: "I make no fewer than forty or fifty pairs, and all at once I put them in a big trough I have, with hot water, and tread on them until they become as they should be." He asked him: "And how do you sell them?" He answered him: "I do not go out of the door of my house. The shopkeepers come to my house and buy from me. They also bring me wool that they buy for me, and I give them a profit for their trouble. And I came to your honor too only for your honor's sake. I do not go out of the door of my house, except to the synagogue. If there is a minyan, I pray with the congregation. And if there is no minyan, I pray alone in my place."

The Baal Shem Tov asked him: "And when you need to marry off your children, what do you do?" He answered: "God helps me through my work, and I marry off my children." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "When you get up before dawn, what do you do?" He said: "Then too I make stockings." He asked him: "And Psalms, how do you say them?" He said to him: "What I can say by heart, I say." The Baal Shem Tov said of him that he was the foundation of the synagogue, until the redeemer comes, speedily in our days, amen. And from this one sees how great is the man who honestly enjoys the labor of his hands.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw from the window a simple craftsman going to the synagogue with a tallit and tefillin, and was deeply shaken. He bought stockings from him, and asked him about his life: the craftsman does not bargain, does not leave his house except to go to the synagogue, always prays, and marries off his children from the labor of his hands. The Baal Shem Tov said that this craftsman was the foundation of the synagogue until the coming of the redeemer.

## 53 — The Honor in Brody

I heard from Rabbi Aaron of Mezhibuzh, who told it before the rabbi of our community: once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to the town of Brody, and stayed the night at a place near Brody. In the night the Baal Shem Tov trembled until his knees knocked against each other, and Rabbi Tzvi the scribe woke from his sleep at the sound of the knocking. The scribe said to him: "What is this trembling?" And the Baal Shem Tov told him: "My master came to me and asked me: 'Who is better, you or Abraham our father?' I asked him: 'What does the question mean?' He said to me: 'You will come to Brody, and they will show you great honor. If you do not stand strong, you will lose everything you have had until now.' And I was very frightened."

When he came to Brody, the rich men came to welcome him, in fine clothes, and he began to play with the horses, stroking them and patting them with his hands, like someone playing with horses. And now you will know how far the Baal Shem Tov's fear of sin reached.

**Summary:** On the way to Brody the Baal Shem Tov trembled all night, for his master came to him and warned him that in Brody they would show him great honor, and if he did not stand strong, he would lose all he had achieved. When the rich men came to honor him, he played with the horses, and the author brings this as an example of his fear of sin.

## 54 — The Dream of Gehinnom

I heard from Rabbi Zelig of Letichev. Although this story is not about the Baal Shem Tov, I write it, because from it one can learn the fear of Heaven. There was a young man, a relative of Rabbi Zelig, who saw in a dream that he was walking with two Hasidim in a field. They said to each other: "Let us go and visit Gehinnom, and see what is done in it." They went and came to Gehinnom, and saw a great gate. They opened it, and saw that this was not yet Gehinnom, and meanwhile they saw that one of them had disappeared. They opened the second gate, and saw that this too was only a passage, and the second one had disappeared, and he was left alone. He hesitated whether to go in or not, and decided: "Since I am already here, I will go in." He saw that this was Gehinnom, and a man was standing by the gate, in tallit and tefillin.

The man asked him: "What are you doing here, and where are you from?" The young man was from Mezhibuzh, and the people of Mezhibuzh always used to say that they were from Bar, because Mezhibuzh was near Bar. He said to him: "I am from Bar, and I came to see Gehinnom." He saw bodies eaten by fire, like "a brand plucked from the fire," heaped up in great piles, like sheaves on the threshing floor. The man said to him: "Since you are from Bar, go to the other side of this pile, and you will see that a young man from Bar is lying there too." And he was very ashamed for him, for that man had been held to be a decent person.

He went back to the gate, and saw that the bodies were lying quietly, and they were not being judged. He asked the man who stood at the gate: "Why are the wicked not being judged today?" The man answered him: "From this I know that you did not say 'Ya'aleh VeYavo' in the evening prayer. You forgot that today is Rosh Chodesh." And so it truly was. Afterward he asked him to let him out. He said to him: "I will certainly not let you out." He asked him again: "Let me out, for I am a decent man." He said to him: "What is your decency? You went to collect alms, and you had evil thoughts." And he explained to him what the thoughts had been. The young man went on and said: "I have to pay school fees." The man began to taste with his lips, and said: "Very fine and very good. Even so, you cannot be let out."

The young man began to beg and plead with him. The man said to him: "I will give you advice: hide under my tallit. Soon the prince of Gehinnom will come here in a great storm, and they will open the gate, and you will slip out through the gate and get out." When the great noise of the prince of Gehinnom was heard from afar, the bodies began to tremble greatly. The young man hid under the man's tallit, and when the prince of Gehinnom came and opened the gate, he slipped out through the gate and got out. A little of the storm wind got into him, and he began to cough, and because of the cough he woke from his sleep. From that day on he had a cough that weakened him for a whole year, and from that illness he died. And the man from Bar, whom he had seen in Gehinnom, lived two more years after this story.

**Summary:** A young man from Mezhibuzh dreamed that he went into Gehinnom, and there he saw a young man from Bar, who was held to be a decent person, lying among the wicked. The gatekeeper, a man in tallit and tefillin, knew that the young man had forgotten to say "Ya'aleh VeYavo" on Rosh Chodesh, and revealed to him his evil thoughts when he collected alms, and did not want to let him out, but in the end hid him under his tallit, and he slipped out when the prince of Gehinnom opened the gate. The storm wind that got into him gave him a cough, and he died a year later, and the man from Bar died two years later.

## 55 — The Informing Against the Tollkeeper

I also heard from Rabbi Zelig, who heard it from Rabbi Shneur, the son of the tzaddik Rabbi Moshe, who was preacher in Rashkov. Rabbi Shneur saw in the town of Ludmir the manuscript of a certain rabbi who wrote a book (*T.Y.A.Y.*), and there it is written that the author saw in a dream that he went into a palace in the Garden of Eden. The palace was very splendid, and he could not have his fill of looking at it, and stayed there because of its great beauty.

Meanwhile he saw that the Satan came into this palace in the form of a dog. A court sat there, and the Satan informed before them against a certain Jew, a village Jew, and said: "It is true that he studies regularly and gives charity. But I have a claim against him: he has lived in that village for several years, and every year he has robbed his gentiles." And he made a reckoning before the court, of a large sum. The court rebuked him, as one rebukes a dog, and he ran away. When the rabbi heard the informing, his heart ached for the Jew, and he did not want to leave until he heard the end. After about half an hour the Satan came a second time and made his claim again, and they rebuked him a second time, and he ran away. And he came back a third time, stood before the court, and said: "Master of the world, I came with a claim before the court, and no one pays attention to my words!" A proclamation went out from above that they should rule on the case. They wrote a ruling for the Satan: that the nobleman of the village would take everything the tollkeeper had, and put him and his household in prison; or that one of his descendants would convert; and the choice was the tollkeeper's. The Satan took the ruling in his mouth, and went.

The rabbi woke from his sleep, and was very grieved for the tollkeeper, for he loved him very much, and especially since he had heard the Satan himself praise him. He sent for the tollkeeper, and told him what had happened. The tollkeeper chose that the nobleman should take everything he had, and put him and his household in prison, and that none of his descendants should convert, Heaven forbid. And so it was: the nobleman took everything he had, and put him and his household in prison. The rabbi had to drag himself along the roads and collect money to ransom these captives, and he ransomed him and his household. From this a person must see how far he must keep from robbing a gentile, as it is written in the books that the Satan collects it from holiness.

**Summary:** A certain rabbi dreamed that in the Garden of Eden the Satan, in the form of a dog, informed three times before the court against a righteous tollkeeper who had robbed the gentiles in his village, until it was ruled that he would lose all his property and be imprisoned with his family, or that one of his descendants would convert. The tollkeeper chose the loss of his property and prison, and so it happened, and the rabbi collected money and ransomed him. From this one learns how far one must keep from robbing a gentile.

## 56 — Rabbi Leibush and the Angel of Death

I heard from the rabbi of our community that Rabbi Leibush of Mezritch was with the Baal Shem Tov for the Days of Awe, and fell ill before Rosh Hashanah. The Baal Shem Tov worked at healing him the whole night of the eve of Rosh Hashanah, and also the night of Rosh Hashanah, and saw on him no sign of death. When the Baal Shem Tov went to the study house at the time of prayer, Rabbi Leibush grew very weak. They told the Baal Shem Tov, and he did not hear, for they were afraid to call out to him loudly. When Rabbi Yitzchak of Mezritch saw that the Baal Shem Tov did not respond, he called out to him in a loud voice. The Baal Shem Tov answered and said: "Why did they not tell me?" And he ran to his house, and found the Angel of Death standing over Rabbi Leibush. He rebuked him sharply, and the Angel of Death fled. The Baal Shem Tov took Rabbi Leibush by the hand, and at once he got well, and he led him to the study house, for he was afraid to leave him at home, in case the Angel of Death came back.

Afterward the Baal Shem Tov said that because of this they had wanted to push him out of this world and the World to Come. And he said that he had done this only because it came upon him suddenly, for he had seen no sign of death before: "And when I suddenly saw the Angel of Death, my heart ached for him, and I did this thing."

**Summary:** Rabbi Leibush fell ill while he was with the Baal Shem Tov on Rosh Hashanah, and during the prayer his condition suddenly grew worse, but they were afraid to disturb the Baal Shem Tov. When Rabbi Yitzchak called out to him, the Baal Shem Tov ran, drove away the Angel of Death that was standing over the sick man, and Rabbi Leibush got well at once. The Baal Shem Tov said afterward that because of this Heaven had wanted to punish him severely, and that he had done it only because the thing took him by surprise.

## 57 — The Rich Man Who Gave Charity in Secret

I heard from the rabbi of our community: there was a man from Mezritch, somewhat rich. When he was getting old, he thought in his heart: "What have I done all the days of my life, and what will become of me? I have spent my years on emptiness and nothing, without Torah and without good deeds." And he did not know what to do. It came into his mind to give charity in secret, and every day he put a thaler (a coin) into the tefillin bag of Rabbi Yitzchak. Rabbi Yitzchak found the money once and again, and said in his heart: "Who is it who puts money into my tefillin bag?" He asked the shammes of the study house to keep an eye out for who was touching his tefillin bag during the prayer, and not to reveal it to anyone but him. The shammes watched for it, until he saw this man touching Rabbi Yitzchak's tallit and tefillin bag. After the prayer he revealed to Rabbi Yitzchak who the man was.

Rabbi Yitzchak began to draw him close, and studied the six orders of the Mishnah with him. Every chapter of Mishnah he studied with him, he went over with him many times, until it was fluent in his mouth, and afterward he studied another chapter with him, until he had finished all the chapters of the Mishnah with him. He also studied *Ein Yaakov* with him in the same way. When he finished the Mishnah and *Ein Yaakov*, the man became something of a scholar. He studied books of the fear of God, books of moral teaching and the book of the Zohar, and bought himself a place in the Mishnah society, and studied until the day of his death. When he fell ill before his death, Rabbi Yitzchak went to visit him, and made him promise with a handshake that he would appear to him at any time.

The custom in that town was that at the time of the death throes all the members of the Mishnah society came, and at the time the soul left they studied Mishnah. And during the thirty days they studied and finished the whole Mishnah for the sake of his soul. After the thirty days the man appeared to Rabbi Yitzchak. Rabbi Yitzchak asked him: "Where is your resting place?" He said: "My resting place is among the righteous, in the Garden of Eden. And truly, I repaired everything in my lifetime. Only one thing remained on me that I did not repair, and the members of the society, by their study for me, repaired it." He came to him at all times, and Rabbi Yitzchak would ask him about passages in the Zohar, and he revealed to him how the passage is studied in the Garden of Eden.

Once Rabbi Yitzchak revealed to a certain man a passage that the dead man had told him, and the dead man did not come to him for a long time. Even so, he was compelled to come, because of the handshake. When he came, Rabbi Yitzchak asked him: "Why were you late in coming? I have not revealed anything to anyone now." He said to him: "Know that they raised me to very high worlds, and it is very bitter for me to go down from those worlds to this world. So I was late in coming, and I was compelled to come only because of the handshake. And so I ask your honor to release me from the handshake." And Rabbi Yitzchak released him.

**Summary:** A rich man, who realized in his old age that he had wasted his life, began to put a coin in secret into Rabbi Yitzchak's tefillin bag, and Rabbi Yitzchak found out who he was, drew him close and taught him Mishnah and *Ein Yaakov*, until he became a scholar. Before his death he promised Rabbi Yitzchak to appear to him, and after his death he came and told him that he was in the Garden of Eden, and taught him how the Zohar is studied there. When they raised him to higher worlds, it was hard for him to come down, and Rabbi Yitzchak released him from his promise.

## 58 — The Judge and the Unfit Tefillin

I also heard from the rabbi of our community: once the son of Rabbi Yosef Yuzpa of Ostroh was taken on as head of the rabbinical court in Polonne, and he brought a judge with him, so that he could stand up against the Mokhiach. On the Sabbath the Mokhiach led the prayers before the ark. Rabbi Yitzchak heard the judge cursing the Mokhiach during the prayer, and said nothing to him. After the prayer, on Sunday after the Sabbath, Rabbi Yitzchak said to him: "I heard clearly that you cursed the Mokhiach, our master." And there was a quarrel between them. In the middle of the quarrel Rabbi Yitzchak looked at him and said: "You are wearing unfit tefillin!" They took the tefillin from the judge's head, took out the parchments, and found that they were unfit. The judge tried hard to make them fit according to the Shulchan Aruch, and Rabbi Yitzchak showed him that he was an ignoramus and did not know the law. And out of great shame the judge fled the town.

**Summary:** A judge who was brought to Polonne to oppose the Mokhiach cursed the Mokhiach during the prayer. When Rabbi Yitzchak argued with him, he looked at him and said that his tefillin were unfit, and so it turned out when they opened them. Rabbi Yitzchak showed that the judge did not know the law, and the judge fled the town in shame.

## 59 — Rabbi Berech's Tefillin

It also happened that Rabbi Yitzchak recognized a flaw in the tefillin on a man's head. Before his death Rabbi Yitzchak was shut in his house because of weakness, and held a minyan in his house. A man of the town prayed there, and after the prayer he studied. It seems to me that his name was Rabbi Berech. Rabbi Yitzchak looked at him and said to him: "Berech, it seems to me that there is a flaw in your tefillin." He took them off his head and gave them to be checked, and they found that they had been unfit from the time they were written. He had had the tefillin since he became bar mitzvah, for they were from a good scribe, and he relied on them. And at that time he was about sixty years old. It was very bitter for him that he had not kept the mitzvah of tefillin all his days, and he begged Rabbi Yitzchak to write tefillin for him. Rabbi Yitzchak promised him, but because of his weakness he put it off from day to day, until he wrote the parchments of the tefillin for him. Afterward Berech went home, and Rabbi Yitzchak said to him: "Do not say only that I have never in my life written tefillin like these; even from the days of Ezra onward there have been no tefillin like these." And about two hours after this he passed from the world. And this Rabbi Yitzchak was the brother of Rabbi Yosef of Kaminka.

**Summary:** Rabbi Yitzchak, when he was ill before his death, looked at a man of sixty who prayed in the minyan at his house, and said that his tefillin were unfit, and it turned out that they had been unfit from the day they were written. The man was sorry that he had not kept the mitzvah of tefillin all his life, and Rabbi Yitzchak wrote him tefillin, and said that there had been no tefillin like these since the days of Ezra. About two hours later he passed away.

## 60 — "Di Frume Rivele"

I heard from the rabbi of our community a story about the mother of the brothers Rabbi Yosef and Rabbi Yitzchak. They lived in the town of Shatanov, and her name was "di frume Rivele" (Rivele the pious). Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to that town. Near the town he saw a light on one of the people of the town. He stood in his place and looked closely, and saw that this light rested on a woman. When he came to the town, and important men came to welcome him, the Baal Shem Tov said to them: "Be ashamed and disgraced, for I saw a light on a woman, and not on a man!" And he asked the people of the town: "Do you know on what woman the light I saw could rest?" They said to him: "Surely it is the woman they call 'di frume Rivele.'" He wanted to send for her, and the men said to him: "There is no need to call her, for surely she herself will come to your honor to ask him for a donation, for she busies herself with good deeds."

The next day, after the prayer, the Baal Shem Tov said: "I see her coming, and she has made up her mind not to take less than forty zlotys from me. You will see what I will do to her." The woman came and said to the Baal Shem Tov: "There are several sick poor people here, so let your honor give a proper donation." He put one small coin before her, and she said nothing, only made motions as if she did not want to accept it. He put another coin before her, and she did not accept it. He said to her: "Who made you a charity collector, and a thief from the charity fund?" And she answered nothing, and did not take offense at it, and the words made no impression on her at all, for she held very strongly to the qualities of humility and lowliness, truly like dust. And she did not leave him, until he had to give her forty zlotys.

Toward evening she came to him and said: "There is a doctor here, and he is very ill. So pray for him that he will live, for he is in great danger." He said: "Then there will be one adulterer fewer in this world." She said to him: "Who says that it is so?" He said to the people who stood there: "Am I telling the truth?" They said: "It is true." She answered and said: "First of all, they did not see the sin with their own eyes (as witnesses must see). And besides, he is an ignorant man, and does not know how grave the transgression is. And if he knew how grave the sin is, he would not commit it." And in truth, the Baal Shem Tov had heard the accusation against the doctor on high, and he repeated the accusation before her, so that she would plead in his favor. And her pleading was accepted on high, and the doctor recovered from his illness. <!-- MATURE: the doctor is called an adulterer; the witnesses standard for adultery is alluded to -->

When she went to her husband's grave, she would knock on the grave with a stick, and say this: "Hershel, Hershel! Go before the Throne of Glory, and pray for your sons that they be poor and needy, so that they do not turn aside from the ways of God."

Once her two sons, Rabbi Yitzchak and Rabbi Yosef, came to her for the Sabbath, to fulfill the mitzvah of honoring their mother, and they arrived on Thursday. The people of the town came to welcome them. They knew one another, for they had been friends in the past, and the old love between them was stirred up. The next day, Friday after the prayer, the mother set the table, gave them food, and sat with them. She said to them: "My sons, I ask one thing of you, that you do for me." They said: "Of course, whatever you command us, we will do and obey." She said to them: "Give me a handshake that you will do my will." They said to her: "Our mother, we are surprised at you, that you suspect us of this." She said to them: "It seems I want to ask a great thing, and I am afraid you will go against my will." They persuaded her not to require a handshake, and promised to do her will. She said to them: "Please, set out on your way at once, and do not spend the Sabbath here. For God gave me sons so that I would have joy from them in the World of Truth, and not in this world." They said to her: "Is it possible that the people of the town will let us leave here on a Friday?" She said to them: "Send your wagons out of the town at once, and I will lead you along the back way of the street, so that the people of the town will not see you." And so they did.

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov came near the town of Shatanov, he saw a light resting on a righteous woman, Rivele the pious, and when she came to ask him for charity for the poor, he insulted her on purpose, and she was not hurt at all, until he gave her forty zlotys. When she asked him to pray for a sick doctor, he said that the doctor was a sinner, and she pleaded in his favor, and the doctor recovered, for the Baal Shem Tov had wanted from the start that she plead in his favor. At her husband's grave she asked that her sons be poor so that they would not turn aside from God's way, and when they came to visit her for the Sabbath, she sent them back in secret on Friday, so that they would not receive honor in her town.

## 61 — The Two Ransomers of Captives

I heard from Rabbi Shmuel the Katzin, who told it before the rabbi: he and Rabbi Ber ben Bunim traveled to ransom captives before Rosh Hashanah, and came to the great Maggid. The Maggid wanted to keep them with him for Rosh Hashanah, and they did not want to, for they said: "We must ransom the captives before the coming Rosh Hashanah, may it come upon us for good." They went on their way, and came to Rabbi Pinchas, and he held them back, and again they did not want to, for the same reason. Rabbi Pinchas said: "Rabbi Shmuel will go back home to ransom the captives, and Rabbi Ber will stay here." Rabbi Shmuel did not want to leave Rabbi Ber. Rabbi Pinchas said: "Rabbi Ber needs to be here, for he has no children." Rabbi Shmuel said: "I need the same thing." He said to him: "You will come for the coming Shemini Atzeret, may it come upon us for good."

Rabbi Ber stayed there for Rosh Hashanah. Before Rosh Hashanah Rabbi Ber fell ill, and could not go to the synagogue to pray on Rosh Hashanah. Rabbi Pinchas came to his house to read the Torah, and called Rabbi Ber up to the Torah. They dressed him and led him to the Torah scroll, and after the second blessing he was restored to his strength. And a daughter was also born to him that year, and she was later Rabbi Pinchas's daughter-in-law. And Rabbi Shmuel came for Shemini Atzeret, and a daughter was born to him too that year.

**Summary:** Two men who were traveling to ransom captives before Rosh Hashanah would not agree to stay for the festival with the Maggid, and afterward with Rabbi Pinchas. Rabbi Pinchas kept Rabbi Ber, who had no children, with him, and told Rabbi Shmuel, who also had no children, to come for Shemini Atzeret. Rabbi Ber fell ill, and was healed when he was called up to the Torah on Rosh Hashanah at Rabbi Pinchas's, and that year a daughter was born to each of the two.

## 62 — The Witch and the Demon

I heard from my father-in-law, and also from the rabbi of our community: when the Baal Shem Tov still kept a tavern in a village, they once needed rain, and he prayed for rain. There was a witch there who had a demon, and she worked witchcraft so that no rain would fall. The Baal Shem Tov, in his prayer, spoiled her witchcraft, and the demon told her that the Baal Shem Tov was spoiling her witchcraft. The witch came to the Baal Shem Tov's mother, and said to her: "Tell your son to leave me alone, or else I will put a spell on him." His mother thought he was quarreling with her over a debt for vodka, and said to him: "My son, leave that gentile woman alone, for she is a witch." He said to his mother: "I am not afraid of her." And he did his part, and prayed for rain.

The witch came to his mother a second time, and afterward she sent her demon against him. When the demon came to him, it could not come within his four cubits. The Baal Shem Tov said to it: "You dared to come to me? Go at once, and harm the gentile woman, through a small window." And so it did. Afterward they caught the demon in a prison in the forest, and it can never move from there. When the Baal Shem Tov had become famous, he was traveling with people through a forest. He stopped, and went, and looked in at the demon sitting in its prison, and laughed a great deal. They asked him, and he told them this story.

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov was still a tavern keeper in a village, he prayed for rain, and with his prayer spoiled the witchcraft of a witch who was holding back the rain. The witch made threats through his mother, and afterward sent her demon against him, but the demon could not come near him, and the Baal Shem Tov sent it to harm the witch herself. The demon was imprisoned in the forest forever, and years later the Baal Shem Tov passed by there, saw it, and laughed.

## 63 — The Fever and the Wine

I heard from Rabbi Yoel, who was preacher in Nemirov: once, when the Baal Shem Tov was traveling, he heard a proclamation: "I lay one of two things upon you: either the Haidamaks will fall upon you and rob you of your money (and he had a sum of two hundred zlotys with him), or you will have a fever." (And the third choice I have forgotten.) The Baal Shem Tov cared about the money, for he had a big creditor at home, and he chose the fever, and so it was. On the Sabbath eve he reached Shargorod, very weak from the fever, and even so he went to the study house and led the prayers before the ark, and the prayer was like a flame of fire. After the prayer they led him to his lodging, holding him up under his arms, because of his weakness. He ordered them to bring him very good wine, drank one cup, and was healed and well at once. And he said jokingly: "It would be good to have the fever one more hour, and drink another cup of wine, for the wine is very good!"

**Summary:** On the road the Baal Shem Tov heard a proclamation that he must choose between the robbing of the money he had with him and a fever, and he chose the fever, for he needed the money for a creditor. In Shargorod he led the prayers before the ark while he was very weak, and his prayer was like fire. Afterward he drank a cup of good wine, and got well at once.

## 64 — The Preacher Who Came After His Death

I heard from Rabbi Falk, head of the rabbinical court of Tetchelnik: once, at the end of the Sabbath, the Baal Shem Tov ordered Rabbi Yosef Ashkenazi, the husband of his stepdaughter, to read *Ein Yaakov* before him, and the Baal Shem Tov lay on the bed and listened to the *Ein Yaakov* from him. At one place the Baal Shem Tov said Torah on a passage of *Ein Yaakov*, and Rabbi Yosef went on reading before him. In the middle of the reading Rabbi Yosef saw the preacher, who was called Rabbi Yosef, who had passed away about three quarters of a year before. He saw him come into the house, dressed in Sabbath clothes, with a hat on his head, and say in a loud voice: "A good week to our master!" And he walked as in his lifetime, with a stick in his hand. When Rabbi Yosef saw him, he was very frightened, and the *Ein Yaakov* fell from his hand. The Baal Shem Tov passed his hand over his face, and he saw him no more.

The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Take a candle in your hand, and go aside." (For he used to say that one candle is harmful to the eyes, and so are three, but two are good for the eyes.) Rabbi Yosef did so, and the Baal Shem Tov stayed with the dead man about half an hour or more, and Rabbi Yosef saw that the Baal Shem Tov was speaking with him. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov called Rabbi Yosef, and said this: "Deitsch (German), come to me!" And he came, and read more *Ein Yaakov* before him. In the middle of the study the Baal Shem Tov began to complain about him, and said to him: "Why were you afraid? Did Rabbi Yosef the preacher slaughter a 'Deitsch' like you in his lifetime, that you were afraid of him after his death?"

Rabbi Yosef asked him: "By what merit did I see him?" He said to him: "Because you read before me, and I spoke before you, and I purified you with my words, and the two of us were bound together, and became one. And because of this you saw him. And if you had strengthened your mind, you would have heard what he spoke with me. And you too would have asked him whatever your heart desired, and he would have answered you. And not only that, but you would have become acquainted with him, and would have seen him always." And Rabbi Yosef was very sorry about it, for it is a level of prophecy, when one sees the soul of a tzaddik, as is written in the book *Sha'arei Kedushah* of Rabbi Chaim Vital. He asked: "Why did the preacher come, and what did he need?" And he said: "For his own need he came."

**Summary:** As Rabbi Yosef Ashkenazi was reading *Ein Yaakov* before the Baal Shem Tov, he suddenly saw a preacher who had passed away three quarters of a year earlier come in and say "A good week," and he was frightened, and the Baal Shem Tov passed a hand over his face and he stopped seeing him. The Baal Shem Tov spoke with the dead man for about half an hour. Afterward he explained to Rabbi Yosef that he had seen the dead man by virtue of the bond between them during the study, and that had he strengthened himself, he could have heard him and spoken with him, and Rabbi Yosef was very sorry that he had missed such a level.

## 65 — The Two Teachers and the Sparks

I heard from Rabbi Yaakov, head of the rabbinical court of Smila, who heard it from his father, Rabbi Meir Charif: once the Baal Shem Tov came to the Rabbi of Polonne (and I am not sure whether the story took place in Nemirov or in Shargorod). He came several days before the Sabbath, and did not eat all those days. The rabbi begged him very much to eat, and he answered: "Let the rabbi excuse me from eating, for there are great sparks here, and they are hard to repair.
