## 145 — The Tollkeeper Who Did Not Come to Town for Passover

Another story (of Rabbi Yechiel): he used to decree that the village people should be in the town for the festival of Passover. And the custom was that he appointed two Torah scholars to go from house to house, and the vessels were kashered in front of them, and so too the matzot were baked in front of them. In one village there was a learned tollkeeper, who paid no attention to the rabbi's decree, and did not come to town for Passover, and his barkeeper did not come to town either. On the intermediate days of the festival the tollkeeper came to town, and the rabbi sent for him, and he came. The rabbi said to him: "Because you did not celebrate Passover in the town on the first days, you will be in the town for Passover on the last days." And so it was: the tollkeeper came to the village, and found that his wife had died. He brought his wife to town on the sixth day of Passover, and until they buried her the time stretched until night, and he had to spend the last day of the festival in the town. And the barkeeper too died at the time of Kiddush on the last day of the festival.

**Summary:** Rabbi Yechiel of Kovel ordered the village people to come to town for Passover, so that they would be careful about the dietary laws, and one tollkeeper did not listen to him. The rabbi told him that because of this he would be in the town on the last days of Passover, and indeed the tollkeeper's wife died, and he had to bring her for burial and spend the last day of the festival in the town. His barkeeper, who had not come either, died at the time of Kiddush.

## 146 — The Sandek Who Refused

A story: there was a head of a rabbinical court in the town of Horodna. There was a circumcision, and he did not want to be sandek. They urged him very much, until he said that the child was a mamzer. The child's father was from a great family, and when the family heard his words, they began to shout, and there was a great outcry in the synagogue. The rabbi said to the shammes: "Go, take my stick in your hand, and go to the mother, and show her the stick, and at once she will confess. And if not, she will die." And she confessed, and said that she had been forced: she went down to the cellar to bring wine, and a nobleman ran after her and raped her. And all of her family fell silent. <!-- MATURE: rape; a child declared a mamzer -->

**Summary:** The head of the rabbinical court of Horodna refused to be sandek at a circumcision, and when they urged him, he revealed that the child had been born of sin, and the father's family rose up against him. The rabbi sent the shammes to show the mother his stick, and she confessed that a nobleman had attacked her by force in the cellar, and then the family fell silent.

## 147 — "A Horse Is a False Hope for Victory"

I heard in the name of Rabbi Eliyahu of Sokolovka: when he was still young, he lived in Mezhibuzh, and once he heard the Baal Shem Tov say: "Whoever wants his prayer to rise to Heaven, let him pray with me word by word." And Rabbi Eliyahu did so: when the Baal Shem Tov said "Lord," he said it too, and when he said "of the world," he too said "of the world," until he finished the whole prayer. And he did so for a long time.

Once, in the Verses of Song, the Baal Shem Tov reached the verse "A horse is a false hope for victory," and he repeated it many, many times, for a very long time. The first time Rabbi Eliyahu said it with him, and afterward he wondered: what is he intending in this verse? He looked in the book *Mishnat Chasidim*, and there were no mystical intentions for it, and he stopped praying with him. Once he came to the Baal Shem Tov's house. The Baal Shem Tov saw him, and said to him: "Eliyahu, you have stopped praying with me." Rabbi Eliyahu told him what had happened, that it was because the Baal Shem Tov had said the verse many times. The Baal Shem Tov answered: "A Jew was caught on the road on a Friday, and could not reach a settlement before the Sabbath, and spent the Sabbath in a field. A robber found out that a Jew was spending the Sabbath in the field, and rode on a horse to find him and kill him. And with those sayings I spoiled his way for him, so that he could not find him."

**Summary:** Rabbi Eliyahu prayed with the Baal Shem Tov word by word, so that his prayer would rise, until once the Baal Shem Tov repeated many times the verse "A horse is a false hope for victory," and Rabbi Eliyahu, who found no mystical intention for it in the books, stopped. The Baal Shem Tov explained to him that with those sayings he had confused a robber who was riding a horse to kill a Jew spending the Sabbath in a field, until he could not find him.

## 148 — There Is No Chance

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in Nemirov, and was walking from the big house to the small house, until he lost his way and went into the cellar. And he ordered the mezuzot to be checked. A man asked him: "And if a person loses his way, must he at once blame it on something? Perhaps it is chance?" The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "With me there is no chance." And from this let every person believe that everything comes by Providence, and let him not put it down to chance, Heaven forbid.

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov lost his way and went into a cellar by mistake, he ordered the mezuzot to be checked. When a man asked him whether perhaps it was only chance, he answered that with him there is no chance, and from this one learns that everything comes by God's Providence.

## 149 — Rabbi Naftali's Amulet

The Rabbi of Polonne had an amulet from Rabbi Naftali the rabbi, and he showed the amulet to the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov recognized in it that it had been written while the writer was in the mikveh and fasting, and said: "I will write an amulet like this after a meal, while I am sitting on the bed."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw an amulet that Rabbi Naftali had written, and recognized that it had been written with immersion and fasting. He said that he himself could write such an amulet after a meal, sitting on the bed.

## 150 — The Tzitzit from Kalisz

This is what I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne: the rabbi chose an old woman for himself, and always gave her a gold coin for four tzitzit threads, doubled to eight, on condition that she would not stop for any speech from the beginning of the spinning to its end. When they first brought tzitzit from the town of Kalisz, he bought them, for they were very beautiful. But he was afraid to put them on his tallit, in case they had not been spun for the sake of the mitzvah. He showed them to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "If you are afraid to put them on your tallit, give them to me, and I will put them on my tallit." And he recognized in them that they had been spun for the sake of the mitzvah.

**Summary:** The Rabbi of Polonne was careful that his tzitzit be spun for the sake of the mitzvah, without a break for speech. When he bought beautiful tzitzit from Kalisz and was afraid they had not been spun for the sake of the mitzvah, the Baal Shem Tov recognized in them that they were fit, and said that he was ready to put them on his own tallit.

## 151 — The Turnip from the Cemetery

I heard: once they put a turnip on the Baal Shem Tov's table, and the Baal Shem Tov did not want to eat. They asked him why, and he said: "This turnip grew in a gentile cemetery." And they did not want to eat it either, and he put it at the end of the table.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov refused to eat a turnip that was served to him, for he sensed that it had grown in a gentile cemetery. The others did not eat it either.

## 152 — The Two Rabbi Davids of Mikolayev

I heard from the rabbi of our community that in the town of Mikolayev there were two Hasidim, and both of them were named Rabbi David. One was old, and we knew him, and one died in his youth. Once they were traveling with the Baal Shem Tov, and they asked him: "Our master, tell us what we need to repair." He told each of them to read before him from a book. One read before him (and I do not know whether Mishnah, *Ein Yaakov* or Zohar). Afterward he told the second to read, and the Baal Shem Tov said: "I know." To one he told his sin in front of his friend. And the second he told to get down from the wagon, and got down with him, and told him in secret, for there was some shame in the matter.

After one of them died, the other Rabbi David mourned for him greatly for many days, for they had loved each other. Once he came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov wanted to comfort him, and said: "Do not worry about your friend, for I was in the palace of King David, and I saw your friend there, and I asked him whether he had repaired the thing he needed to repair, and he said that he had already repaired it in his lifetime. And I saw the sage Rabbi Akiva standing on guard, in red garments, with a spear in his hand."

**Summary:** Two Hasidim both named Rabbi David asked the Baal Shem Tov to know what they needed to repair, and he told each of them his sin, one in front of his friend and the other in secret. When one of them died young and the other mourned for him greatly, the Baal Shem Tov comforted him: he had seen his friend in the palace of King David, and the friend had told him that he had already repaired what he needed in his lifetime.

## 153 — The Angel Hadarniel

I also heard from that rabbi that the Baal Shem Tov saw the angel Hadarniel, who is the second angel Moses our teacher met when he went up Mount Sinai, as is written in the holy Zohar.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw the angel Hadarniel, whom, according to the Zohar, Moses our teacher met when he went up Mount Sinai.

## 154 — The Young Man Killed for the Sanctification of God's Name

I heard from the rabbi of our community that once a young man in Polonne was killed for the sanctification of God's name, after false accusations were made against him, and he sanctified God's name greatly. Once the Baal Shem Tov came to Polonne, and stood by the house of the young man's mother, and sent for her. She came out, and he comforted her, and said to her: "Know that at the hour of his killing all the worlds were opened that were opened to Isaac at the Binding." And he was afraid to go into the house, for the house had earlier been a gentile dwelling, and he said that because of this house the young man had been killed.

**Summary:** A young man from Polonne was killed for the sanctification of God's name after a false accusation. The Baal Shem Tov comforted his mother, and told her that at the hour of his death all the worlds were opened that were opened to Isaac at the Binding. He did not want to go into her house, for he said that because of the house, which had earlier belonged to gentiles, the young man had been killed.

## 155 — Rabbi Gershon in Acre

I heard from that rabbi that once the Baal Shem Tov said: "I wonder: on Friday night, during the prayer, I looked for Rabbi Gershon, and did not find him in the Land of Israel. And on Sabbath morning, during the prayer, I saw him in the Land of Israel. And I do not know what to think of it, unless he went beyond the Sabbath boundary." Some years later Rabbi Gershon came to him, and the Baal Shem Tov asked him about this, and Rabbi Gershon did not remember. Afterward he remembered: they had given him the honor of a circumcision in Acre, and he spent the Sabbath there. "And in Acre there are two synagogues, one outside the Land of Israel and one in the Land of Israel. I went and prayed on Friday night in the synagogue outside the Land, and afterward my heart ached over it: why had I not prayed in the Land of Israel? And the next day I went and prayed in the synagogue in the Land of Israel."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov wondered that on Friday night he did not find Rabbi Gershon in the Land of Israel, and in the morning he did find him there. Years later Rabbi Gershon remembered that on that Sabbath he had been in Acre for a circumcision, and on Friday night he had prayed in a synagogue that counts as outside the Land of Israel, and in the morning he had moved to the synagogue inside the Land of Israel.

## 156 — Telling the Praises of the Tzaddikim

(And a story that happened with the head of the rabbinical court of Horodna, at a circumcision.)

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne and from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov said: "Whoever tells the praises of the tzaddikim, it is as if he were engaged in the Work of the Chariot." I also heard that in whatever world a person is, it is as if the worlds go before him.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov said that whoever tells praises of tzaddikim, it is as if he were engaged in the highest secrets of the Torah, the "Work of the Chariot."

## 157 — The Angels and the Demons, and the Demon That Was Sent

I was once in Tulchin with a man named Rabbi David. At that time the match was made between the pious Rabbi Baruch and the pious Rabbi Yosef, the son of Rabbi Yechiel Michel. And Rabbi David heard from Rabbi Yosef, in the name of the Baal Shem Tov, on the verse "And Jacob sent angels before him" (the Hebrew word means both messengers and angels): these are the angels who go before him. And he said that before Jacob our father there always go angels on his right and demons on his left. And before the Baal Shem Tov too there go angels on his right and demons on his left. And he did not want to make use of the angels, because they are holy, nor of the demons, because they are liars.

Once a widow lived in a village, and a man came to take the *arenda* (the lease) from her. He went to the nobleman to lease the arenda. One of the demons said to the Baal Shem Tov: "Send me to him." And the Baal Shem Tov did not want to. The demon begged him very much to send it, and promised him that it would do as he commanded it. The Baal Shem Tov sent it, and commanded it: "Take care, do him no harm. Only when he wants to speak before the nobleman, stop his mouth with your hand." And the demon let him say all he wanted, and waited until he had taken the arenda and received the contract. And when he came back, and reached the bridge, it harmed him, and he fell on the bridge. The Baal Shem Tov ran and came, and decreed on the demon that it come out of him at once without any harm in the world. The demon answered and said: "See, my master, that I obeyed you as you commanded me." And I do not remember, and it seems to me that the man got up healthy and strong.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov said that, as before Jacob our father, so before him angels go on the right and demons on the left, but he did not make use of them, for the angels are holy and the demons are liars. Once, when a man came to take a lease from a widow, the Baal Shem Tov agreed to send a demon to stop his mouth before the nobleman, but the demon waited until the man had received the contract, and only then threw him down on a bridge. The Baal Shem Tov drove the demon out of him, and the demon claimed that it had done exactly as it was commanded.

## 158 — The Man Who Took the Village from the Widow

And from my father-in-law, Rabbi Alexander, I heard a similar story: there was a man who wanted to take a village from a widow. The Baal Shem Tov reproved him, and he did not listen to him. He rode on a horse past the Baal Shem Tov's house, knocked on the window and said: "See, I am going to lease the village, and I will see what God will do to me!" And the Baal Shem Tov shed tears. When the man came to the village, he leased the arenda at a large increase, and the nobleman meant to write him the contract. Meanwhile his head began to ache, and he said to the nobleman: "My head aches very much!" He said to him: "Sit down, I will not be long writing the contract." And he waited a little. And he said again: "My head aches very much!" The nobleman said to him: "Lie down on the bed." And foam began to come out of his mouth, and the nobleman ordered his servant to throw him among the pigs, as they say: "They draw a man near only when they benefit from him, and do not stand by him in his hour of need." They brought him home, and his hands and feet were paralyzed, and his tongue too was taken from him. They called the Baal Shem Tov to heal him, and he began to speak a little. Meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov sensed that his household were seeking help from gentile witches to heal him, and from then on he did not want to go to him, and the man could speak only a few words.

**Summary:** A man who wanted to take a village from a widow did not listen to the Baal Shem Tov's reproof, and even knocked on his window mockingly, and the Baal Shem Tov wept. At the nobleman's, just before the contract was signed, he got a headache and a stroke, and the nobleman threw him among the pigs. The Baal Shem Tov began to heal him, but when he found out that his household were also seeking help from witches, he stopped, and the man was left almost without speech.

## 159 — Rabbi Israel Shapir

I heard from the rabbi of our community: there was a man in Mohilev named Rabbi Israel Shapir, and they informed against him to the lord. He traveled to the lord, and turned off the road to the Baal Shem Tov, to tell him his affair and ask him for help. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Do not be afraid of it. But every person must be held (in prison) some time." He traveled to the lord in fear, and when he came to him, he found favor in his eyes, and the lord even lent him more money. On the way back from the lord he passed through the town of Bar, and the lord of the town took him into prison over some matter. The people of the town said to the lord: "Leave him, for he is a man who can pursue you." And the lord released him to go home in peace.

**Summary:** Rabbi Israel Shapir, who had been informed against, came to the Baal Shem Tov before traveling to the lord, and the Baal Shem Tov told him not to be afraid, but that every person must be in prison once. The lord received him well, and on the way back he was imprisoned for a short time by the lord of the town of Bar, and released.

## 160 — The Mokhiach and the Rabbi of Bar

A story: there was a head of a rabbinical court in the town of Bar, and one of the people of the town quarreled with him, and traveled to the lord to complain about him (I do not know whether over the rabbinate or over something else). He turned off the road to the Baal Shem Tov, so that he would pray for him to get what he wanted from the lord, and the Baal Shem Tov promised him, and he went on his way. The Mokhiach was in Mezhibuzh at that time, and knew that the rabbi was in the right against the people of the town, and that the opponent was a scoundrel, and he was afraid to tell this to the Baal Shem Tov. What did the Mokhiach do? He gathered a minyan to himself in secret, took a Torah scroll, and canceled the judgments over the rabbi, so that they could not do him harm.

Once (it seems to me it was Rosh Chodesh) the Mokhiach was sitting with his relative by marriage, and it seems to me it was Rabbi Zev Kutzes, at the Baal Shem Tov's table. In the middle of the meal the Baal Shem Tov said: "I do not know what to think of one thing: I prayed for a man from Bar, and it is as if they answer me, and do not answer me." The Mokhiach began to laugh a little. His relative said to him: "What was it that I saw, that they called a minyan of ten to you?" And the Mokhiach evaded and did not answer. The Baal Shem Tov pressed him to tell the story, and the Mokhiach answered: "I will not hide it from our master: that man from Bar spoke well before your honor, and I know the inside of the matter, that the rabbi is in the right against the people of the town. And I gathered a minyan, and took a Torah scroll, and canceled the judgments from him." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "May blessing rest on your head! You did well!"

**Summary:** A man from Bar asked the Baal Shem Tov to pray for his success before the lord against the town rabbi, and the Baal Shem Tov promised. The Mokhiach, who knew that the rabbi was right and the man was wicked, gathered a minyan in secret with a Torah scroll and canceled the judgments over the rabbi. When the Baal Shem Tov wondered that his prayer was not being accepted, the Mokhiach admitted what he had done, and the Baal Shem Tov blessed him and said he had done well.

## 161 — Rabbi Moshe the Cantor on Rosh Hashanah

I heard from Rabbi Yakil of Mezhibuzh that in that town there was a cantor of the study house named Rabbi Moshe, and he was a wondrous tzaddik. Once they gave him a prayer to lead on Rosh Hashanah in the study house (I do not know whether the morning prayer or Musaf of the second day). And there was a prayer leader who had that prayer on the first day, and he told Rabbi Moshe to exchange the prayer with him, and Rabbi Moshe would lead the prayer on the first day. And so they did. Rabbi Moshe led the prayer on the first day, and in the middle of the cantor's repetition Rabbi Moshe turned his face to the congregation, and addressed that prayer leader by name: "How could you set me up as a target for the arrow?" And he spoke words of reproof for about half an hour, and there was great weeping in the synagogue. And afterward he went back to his prayer. The scholars were angry with him for stopping in the middle of the Amidah. After the afternoon prayer, when they went to say Tashlich, Rabbi Yakil asked him: "Why did you stop in the middle of the Amidah?" And Rabbi Moshe knew nothing of it, and did not remember anything. Rabbi Yakil said to him: "I would not have believed this thing, but because I heard about the Baal Shem Tov that he fell upon the holy ark and afterward did not remember, I believed that this too could be so."

**Summary:** Rabbi Moshe, the righteous cantor of the study house in Mezhibuzh, stopped on Rosh Hashanah in the middle of the cantor's repetition, turned to the congregation and spoke words of reproof for half an hour, and the congregation wept. When he was asked afterward why he had stopped, he remembered nothing. Rabbi Yakil believed him, for he had heard that the Baal Shem Tov too once fell upon the holy ark and did not remember.

## 162 — The Livelihood of the Rabbi of Rashkov

I heard from the rabbi of our community: when the Rabbi of Polonne was head of the rabbinical court in Rashkov, the angels said to the Baal Shem Tov: "Why are you silent, when the rabbi of Rashkov has no livelihood?"

**Summary:** When the Rabbi of Polonne was rabbi in Rashkov and had no livelihood, the angels told the Baal Shem Tov that he must not keep silent about it.

## 163 — The Light on the Mohel's Face

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne that when Rabbi Moshe the cantor circumcised a Jewish baby, a light could be seen on his face until the third day. And the rabbi of our community told him that his brother (Rabbi Moshe's) was a shochet in Kitov, and that Rabbi Moshe too was from that area.

**Summary:** When Rabbi Moshe the cantor circumcised a baby, a light was seen on his face for three days.

## 164 — The Redemption of the Firstborn, and Moses Our Teacher

I heard that once the Baal Shem Tov sat at the feast of a redemption of the firstborn. At the end of the meal the Baal Shem Tov took a piece of bread and chewed it, and his face became red, and his manner changed, and the people thought he was choking, and wanted to save him. Rabbi Zev Kutzes looked into his face and said: "Leave him alone." And so it went for a long time, and he came back to himself on his own. They asked him: "What was that?" He answered: "When I ate the piece of bread, I concentrated on the mystical intentions of eating, and I reached in one intention what Moses our teacher intended when Zipporah gave him food to eat for the first time. And when I concentrated on this unification, Moses our teacher came here, and what happened happened."

**Summary:** At the feast of a redemption of the firstborn the Baal Shem Tov chewed a piece of bread, and his face grew so red that they thought he was choking, but Rabbi Zev Kutzes said to leave him alone. When he came back to himself, he explained that in eating he had concentrated on what Moses our teacher intended when Zipporah gave him food for the first time, and that Moses our teacher came to him at that moment.

## 165 — The Wagon from Olyka, and the Birth of Rabbi Yosef of Yampol

I heard, and I do not remember from whom I heard: when the famous pious Rabbi Yechiel Michel was with the Baal Shem Tov, once, on Friday morning, before the morning prayer, the Baal Shem Tov told him to travel home that day. Rabbi Yechiel Michel did not answer him, for he said in his heart: "Today is the Sabbath eve, and I do not yet have a wagon." And he went to the study house to read the weekly portion twice in the Hebrew and once in the Aramaic translation. When it was time to eat, the Baal Shem Tov's wife asked: "Where is Rabbi Michel?" The Baal Shem Tov said: "I ordered him to travel home." Even so, she sent for him, and they found him in the study house, and called him to come and eat. When he came, the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Sit down and eat, and you will have a wagon."

After the meal he went outside, and saw a young man standing with a wagon in the market, with three horses. He asked him: "Where are you from?" He said: "From the town of Olyka." And so it truly was: the young man had left the merchant at some place, and was going back home with the empty wagon. And they said that from Mezhibuzh to Olyka there are seventeen parsahs. The driver asked him in what place or what settlement he would spend the Sabbath, and he answered that he did not know how it would come about that he would get there for the Sabbath. Rabbi Yechiel Michel hired the driver, and went to take leave of the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Go home in peace, and you will find your wife in a hard labor, and you will find many women there. Send them out of your house, and whisper in her ear what I have taught you, and you will have a son in a good hour." And so it was: he reached home two hours before nightfall, and did as the Baal Shem Tov had commanded him, and his wife gave birth to a son. And this child is Rabbi Yosef of Yampol.

**Summary:** On Friday morning the Baal Shem Tov told Rabbi Yechiel Michel to travel home, and he did not go because he had no wagon, and the Baal Shem Tov promised him that he would have one. After the meal he found in the market an empty wagon going back exactly to his town, and he arrived before the Sabbath, a distance of seventeen parsahs. As the Baal Shem Tov had told him, his wife was in a hard labor, and he whispered to her what he had been taught, and she gave birth to Rabbi Yosef of Yampol.

## 166 — What "Enclothing" Is

I heard from the Rabbi of Polonne that he asked the Baal Shem Tov about the matter of "enclothing" that is mentioned in the upper worlds: how does this thing exist, as an example, in a person? And he said to him: "When you give your fellow benefit, whether with money or with Torah, that is enclothing: one clothes the other, the giver the receiver, or the other way around."

**Summary:** The Rabbi of Polonne asked the Baal Shem Tov what the "enclothing" spoken of in the upper worlds means. The Baal Shem Tov explained that when a person gives to his fellow, money or Torah, the giver "clothes" the receiver.

## 167 — The Mikveh

I also heard from him that the Baal Shem Tov asked him: "Why do you stay so long in the mikveh? And I, when I go to the mikveh, I close my eyes once, and see all the worlds."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov asked the Rabbi of Polonne why he stayed so long in the mikveh, and said that he himself, when he immerses, closes his eyes once and sees all the worlds.

## 168 — The Boy from the Slaughterhouse

I heard from Rabbi Avraham of Mezhibuzh, the son-in-law of Rabbi Michel of Tulchin, that Rabbi Yosef, the preacher of Mezhibuzh, fell ill before his death with an illness of the bowels, and he had no one to look after him properly. Once a boy from the slaughterhouse came to him, and took pity on him, and took the sheet from under him, and cleaned him well, and washed the sheet in the river, and dried it, and spread it under him. And so he did every day, until the day the preacher died. Not many days passed, and this boy fell ill. The preacher came (after his death) to the Baal Shem Tov, and said to him: "Why do you not go to visit my beloved one, who is ill?" And at once the Baal Shem Tov went with his people to visit the sick boy. The boy passed from the world, and the Baal Shem Tov saw the preacher walking in front of his bier.

**Summary:** A simple boy from the slaughterhouse looked after the preacher of Mezhibuzh devotedly every day when he fell ill before his death and there was no one to clean him. A short time later the boy fell ill, and the departed preacher came to the Baal Shem Tov and asked him to visit "his beloved one." When the boy passed away, the Baal Shem Tov saw the preacher walking in front of his bier.

## 169 — A Sign for Names

I heard from the son of the tzaddik Rabbi Chaim Krasner that once Rabbi Chaim asked the Baal Shem Tov to remember his name and his wife's name, to pray for them. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "And how shall I remember your names?" Rabbi Chaim answered him: "Let us make a sign: 'Chaim ben Rachel,' its initials are 'ChBR,' *chaver* (a companion). And 'the wife of a companion is like a companion': 'Chaya bat Rivkah,' its initials are also 'ChBR.'" The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "What kind of a sign is that? Perhaps the Ch is Chaikel or something like it, and the Ch of her name is Chana or something like it?" And the Baal Shem Tov said: "There is another sign: 'Chaim' and 'Chaya' in gematria are like the names Havayah and Adonai."

**Summary:** Rabbi Chaim asked the Baal Shem Tov to pray for him and his wife, and suggested a sign for remembering their names by their initials. The Baal Shem Tov said that initials are a weak sign, for the same letter can fit other names, and suggested a better sign: the gematria of "Chaim" and "Chaya" together equals two names of God.

## 170 — "Nachman the Madman"

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the famous Rabbi Nachman of Kosov held a village on lease. He used to send to the town, to the members of the holy circle, to each and every one what he needed to repair in this world of his sins, and it was all true. But they were very angry at this prophecy, for there was an agreement among them that none of them would prophesy. They wanted to send one of them to ask what this was, and the Rabbi of Polonne answered: "I will travel to him, for I need to travel to him anyway, and I will ask him." (For he was the teacher of Rabbi Nachman's sons, and they ate at his table, and Rabbi Nachman used to send him flour and grain, and for a long time he had not sent him any.)

The Mokhiach traveled to him, and did not find him at home. He saw a wagon ready, and asked about him, and they told him he had gone to the village, and he waited for him. When Rabbi Nachman came, the Mokhiach saw him carrying a great load of straw on his shoulders. The Mokhiach said this to him: "Nachman the madman! You yourself have to carry straw? You should have ordered your gentile servant to carry this straw!" And Rabbi Nachman answered him: "You are the madman! The Torah said 'And I will give grass in your field for your cattle,' and only afterward 'and you shall eat and be satisfied.' And you say to honor the gentile servant with this mitzvah?" And the Mokhiach was silent, for Rabbi Nachman had spoken well. Rabbi Nachman's horses were harnessed to the wagon, to travel to the town. The Mokhiach said to him: "Why have you not sent me flour?" He answered him: "See, the wagon is ready, so that I can travel myself with the flour for you."

Afterward he asked him: "What is this, that you prophesy?" And he answered him: "I am no prophet, nor a prophet's son. You know the story: I sat at the third Sabbath meal with Rabbi Moshe the rabbi, head of the rabbinical court of Kitov, and they lingered until well into the night. The shochet was one of the members of the circle, and he too sat there. A butcher came, named Leib, who was called Leib Glitz, and called the shochet to come and slaughter, once, twice and three times, and the shochet did not want to go, for he longed to hear the words of the living God. And this was always their conversation: how to serve God. When the butcher saw that the shochet did not want to go and leave there, he said in his heart: 'I will come and disturb them, and they will pray the evening prayer, and the shochet will go and slaughter.' And so he did: he brought a burning candle. The rabbi was angry with him and put him under a ban, and the butcher died.

"I saw in a dream that I was walking past a synagogue, and I heard someone preaching words of Torah, and I ran to the synagogue. When I reached the door of the synagogue, I recognized that it was a *kelipah* (for the rabbi said that they had a sign to know whether it was the dead man himself or not). I ran at it, and my father held me back, and said: 'Will you shame a person again?' (For there had been a story, in real life, that my father-in-law once dragged a preacher away from his sermon.) I slipped out of my father's hand, and spat in its face, and it was left standing naked, with only a cloth to cover it. I recognized that it was the butcher, and I said to him: 'Leib! Where did you get the words of Torah from?' And he answered: 'Because of the ban they do not judge me, and I go around the Garden of Eden and hear what they study inside, and I cannot reach either Gehinnom or the Garden of Eden. So I ask your honor to tell the rabbi to release me from the ban.' And he told me about each and every one what he needs to repair, and that is what I sent you.

"I asked: 'And what do I have to repair?' And he led me to a house, and showed me a gentile lying dead, and said to me: 'This you must repair.'" And the story was this: once Rabbi Nachman was traveling, and stayed the night in a village, and did not want to stay with a Jew (and his reason I did not know). He gave a bottle of vodka to that gentile, and stayed with him, and gave fodder to his horses. At night he heard from the gentile things between him and his wife, and could not keep himself from an impure thought, and had a nocturnal emission, God save us. He fasted for it from Sabbath to Sabbath, and killed the *kelipah*, but the mark still remained. "And the butcher said that this is what I must repair. I began to weep, and afterward I saw something that looked like a sword hanging on the wall. I took the sword and cut the gentile in two, and the butcher said that with this I had already repaired it. So I wanted to travel to you. And since you have come to me, I no longer need to travel, and you will see that they will release the butcher from the ban. And here is flour, and go in peace." <!-- MATURE: sexual content (overheard marital talk, nocturnal emission); a naked figure; cutting a body in two in a dream -->

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Kosov sent his companions what each of them needed to repair, and they were angry that he was "prophesying," and sent the Mokhiach to ask him. The Mokhiach found him carrying straw for the animals himself, and Rabbi Nachman explained that it is a mitzvah to feed the animal first. About the prophecy he told that the butcher who had been put under a ban and died came to him in a dream, asked that the ban be lifted from him, and revealed to him what each one needed to repair, and also what Rabbi Nachman himself needed to repair, and that in the dream itself he had already repaired it.

## 171 — The Flight Before the Sabbath

I heard from my father-in-law: once, before the first flight (which was before the flight that happened in our days), on the Sabbath eve after midday, while everyone was cooking in honor of the Sabbath, the Baal Shem Tov sent word to all the people of the town that they should all flee. And he fled at once, so as to welcome the Sabbath early, as was his custom. He had good horses, and the way was not far, only one parsah to the river Dniester. He crossed the river and welcomed the Sabbath. And all the people of the town strained the soup from the meat, and fled across the Dniester too, only a little later, for they had to light the Sabbath candles at the crossing. And thank God, they were all saved. And on the Sabbath day the robbers came to the town, and did not find the Jews. And I do not know whether the story happened in Tluste or in Kalimina, for afterward the Baal Shem Tov lived in Kalimina.

**Summary:** Once, on Friday afternoon, while everyone was cooking for the Sabbath, the Baal Shem Tov sent word to all the people of the town to flee at once, and he himself fled across the river Dniester. All the people of the town fled after him, and lit the Sabbath candles at the crossing. On the Sabbath the robbers reached the town, and did not find a single Jew there.

## 172 — The Leaseholders of Slutsk

I heard from the rabbi of our community: the Baal Shem Tov was with the leaseholders (the *derzhavtses*) of Slutsk. One was named Rabbi Shmuel, and the other Rabbi Gedaliah, and Rabbi Shmuel's wife was named Tovele. She urged her husband to send for the Baal Shem Tov, for they had built a house of stone, and were afraid to live in it. They sent for him, and he came to them. The men were not at home, and she wanted to make a feast, and the Baal Shem Tov said that he needed to see the knife. The shochet had to come and show him the knife, and the people of the town were very angry: how did he dare to check the knife of the town's shochet, and in a great community at that? But they could do nothing to him, for the mistress had ordered it.

At the afternoon prayer they heard that he prayed in the Sephardi rite, and they were even angrier. When Rabbi Shmuel came home, they informed against the Baal Shem Tov to him, and he answered them: "It is not proper to do anything to him without a reason." Before the afternoon prayer too the woman had taken him to see the buildings, and in several places he made movements, as if he were seeing something, and they suspected him of lying, for literally all the people of the town were there. Rabbi Shmuel told his wife that he wanted to test the Baal Shem Tov in one thing (and I have forgotten what the thing was). The Baal Shem Tov heard from his lodging what Rabbi Shmuel was saying to her in bed, and sent the servant for her to come at once, and not to fear the guards standing there. At once she got out of bed and went, and her husband tried to stop her from going, but she paid no attention to him and went. When she came, the Baal Shem Tov hinted at him: "The fool wants to test me!" And she was very frightened, and appeased the Baal Shem Tov until he was appeased.

When she came back home, she told her husband, and the Baal Shem Tov became important in his eyes. The next day he made a great feast for him, and spoke with him about the journey to the Holy Land, for he wanted to send him by the land route. He asked the Baal Shem Tov how much expense this would need, and the Baal Shem Tov answered that sixty thousand would be needed for expenses. He answered him: "That is a small thing for me." And he promised him that he would send him money every year for his living. And the Baal Shem Tov thought that he was not worried about his living, for the main thing is to merit to be there, in the Holy Land. The Baal Shem Tov stayed there about three weeks.

Once the woman Tovele asked the Baal Shem Tov: "How long will our good fortune last?" And the Baal Shem Tov rebuked her, and told her that one does not ask such things. She urged him very much, and he closed his eyes for one moment, and said: "It will last twenty-two years." When her husband heard this, he was filled with fury against him. And another thing happened: one of the rich men had a sick child, and he came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov said that the child would live. Afterward he saw that the child would die, and he understood that they would want to shame him. He left there at midnight, by the shortening of the road, about fifteen parsahs, until he left their territory for another kingdom. They came to a village and left the village, and inside the village or the town he drove like anybody, and when they left that village, they came at once to another village. Rabbi Tzvi the scribe asked him: "What is this?" And the Baal Shem Tov did not answer him at all, for it is forbidden to stop, but only pushed him away with his hand.

So I heard from that rabbi several times, that it is forbidden to stop. When they had fled across the border, and stopped to rest, the Baal Shem Tov wrote a letter: "Know for certain that I could have found a way for myself, so that you could do nothing to me. Only I was afraid that it would harm me in another place, and so I went away from you. And not one of my words will fall to the ground, as I said: after twenty-two years you will have a downfall at the hands of your servant." And he signed at the end: "So says Israel Baal Shem Tov, servant of God." And he sent the letter with a special messenger. Rabbi Shmuel was very angry, and was ashamed to show the letter to others, and only showed the signature.

**Summary:** The rich leaseholders of Slutsk invited the Baal Shem Tov to their new stone house, and the people of the town were angry with him for checking the shochet's knife and praying in the Sephardi rite. When Rabbi Shmuel wanted to test him, the Baal Shem Tov heard his words from afar, and the woman appeased him, and Rabbi Shmuel even promised to pay for his journey to the Land of Israel. But when the Baal Shem Tov told the woman that their good fortune would last twenty-two years, and when he understood that a sick child he had said would live was going to die, he fled at night by the shortening of the road, and sent a letter in which he repeated that in twenty-two years a downfall would come upon Rabbi Shmuel at the hands of his servant.

## 173 — The Wagon That Went Through the Gate, and the End of the Leaseholders of Slutsk

I heard from other people that the Baal Shem Tov reached home in three days, by the shortening of the road, although much rain fell, for this story was before Rosh Hashanah, and he wanted to be at home for the festival. And I heard from a man of our community named Rabbi Eliezer, who was from the town of Birtse, that on his way back the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath near that town, a short parsah away. Rabbi Eliezer told me the name of the village, and I do not remember it. The Baal Shem Tov came to the village, and when they said in the house of the leaseholder that the Baal Shem Tov had come, the leaseholder ran out to meet him, and was very glad, for he had never had children, and he said in his heart: "Perhaps I will have salvation." He greeted him. The Baal Shem Tov asked: "Is it possible for me to spend the Sabbath with you?" And he answered him: "Of course it is possible, and I very much ask your honor to spend the Sabbath with me." The Baal Shem Tov asked: "And will you have a minyan?" They counted the people who had come with him, and there were only nine. The leaseholder said to the Baal Shem Tov: "We have only nine men." The Baal Shem Tov answered: "There will be a minyan."

The Baal Shem Tov did not move from the wagon, and ordered them to drive the wagon into the covered gateway of the house. The gate was very low, and the gate had no special lintel, only a beam of the wall that lay across the two doorposts. And the hood of the wagon was high. When the driver drove into the gateway, he stopped the horses before the hood reached the beam. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Why did you stop?" The driver drove on, and the lintel rose by itself with the whole building, and the wagon went in with the hood. And about this I did not ask how he got out of there afterward. And all the people stood and saw this wonder, and the leaseholder was very glad, and said in his heart: "God willing, I will have a cure."

When the time after midday came, the Baal Shem Tov said to gather the minyan, to stand up to pray, as was always his custom to pray on the Sabbath eve right after midday. He said to them: "There are ten men among us." They answered: "There are only nine men." He said: "I know that there will certainly be a minyan." And they laughed behind his back, for they saw that there were not ten there. Meanwhile it came into the leaseholder's mind that across the river there was a small lease, and there was a man there who had been sick for about ten years already, without the use of his hands and feet, and without speech, and he lay like a silent stone, and they only fed him a little. The master of the house said to the Baal Shem Tov: "Perhaps you mean him?" The Baal Shem Tov said: "Go, call him to come here for the minyan." And he gave them his stick, for him to take it in his hand and come. They went to him, and he did not get up and did not move, and they came back empty-handed. The Baal Shem Tov took the hat off his head, and sent them, and said: "Put my hat on him, and put the stick in his hand, and he will come." And so it was: this man came to the minyan, and lived ten years after this, and was a big, strong man, like a giant.

And Rabbi Eliezer saw this gate twenty years later, and they showed him the sign: the lintel had still not gone back to its first place. When the leaseholder saw the two awesome wonders, and they sat down to eat, he began to plead with the Baal Shem Tov, and said: "I am already in the middle of my years, and I have no children, and in a while I will be old, and no longer fit to have children. What will become of me in the end?" The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "You will have children." He asked him whether to divorce his wife. He answered him: "What is that to you? You will have children." After this he lived with his wife until he reached the age of sixty, and his wife died. He married another woman, and she bore him two sons. And I do not know whether they were twins or one after the other.

And the end of the leaseholders (the *derzhavtses*) of Slutsk I heard from Rabbi Yitzchak Isaac, like this: the duke of Slutsk was Radziwill. He borrowed a sum of money from them, and they could not collect it from him, and they were afraid he would ask them for another loan. It came into their minds to settle in the city of Breslau. They spoke with the king about taking them under his rule in Breslau, and gave him the debt as a gift, for the king to collect it from the duke and his estates. And so they did. They set out by ship on the water, and when they came opposite the town of Slonim, where the duke sat, they took with them three barrels of silver, to deposit them in Breslau, and their clerk traveled with them too. When they came near the harbor, Rabbi Shmuel went with the clerk to his lodging, and dressed up to go before the lord. While he was dressing up and putting on his clothes, the clerk disappeared from him. Rabbi Shmuel was very frightened, and ran quickly to the duke.

When he saw the clerk standing with the duke in the inner room and speaking with him, he came up to him, and struck the clerk on the cheek. The duke answered: "That you struck him before me, although it is not proper, I forgive you, because you are sure of my great love for you, that even if you struck my son before me, I would forgive you. But give an answer to his words." And Rabbi Shmuel had no answer. The duke sent to the ship, and found it as the clerk had said. At once Rabbi Shmuel was taken into prison, and the duke ordered soldiers (dragoons) with an officer (a major) to be sent to Slutsk, and ordered the officer to seal (confiscate) everything they had, the money and the goods.

When the letter came from the duke, terror fell on the officer's heart, and he said: "Perhaps the wheel will turn in their favor, and I will have an enemy." He made himself drunk, and slept a very long time, until night. When he saw that the whole town was asleep, the officer himself went to the woman Tovele, Rabbi Shmuel's wife, and told her about it, and that she should clear out and take away whatever she could, for in the morning everything would be confiscated. She sent her son-in-law, and he fled with the jewels, and afterward became head of the rabbinical court in Breslau, and from this he grew very rich, and was a great rich man. And the duke seized all the members of their family he could find, for they were forced to write to all their relatives a letter of invitation to a wedding. Those who heard of it on the way turned back, and whoever did not hear was seized.

Tovele asked the people of their town to send to the Baal Shem Tov; perhaps he would help them with his prayer. The people of the town hated them, and even so they sent a special messenger, and put their words in the messenger's mouth in complete secrecy: that he should ask the Baal Shem Tov not to help them. Two or three days passed, until the messenger revealed the request of the people of the town. The Baal Shem Tov said: "I had cleared a certain way in which I could help them. But since Jews are asking that they not be helped, I will not help them." And this happened after twenty-two years had passed, as the Baal Shem Tov had written to him.

**Summary:** On the way back from Slutsk the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath in a village with a childless leaseholder, and his wagon went in through a low gate as the lintel rose by itself, and a paralyzed man who had lain for ten years got up and came to complete the minyan, wearing the Baal Shem Tov's hat and holding his stick. The Baal Shem Tov promised the leaseholder that he would have children, and after many years, from a second wife, two sons were born to him. And the prophecy in the letter to the leaseholders of Slutsk came true twenty-two years later: their clerk informed against them to the duke, Rabbi Shmuel was imprisoned and their property confiscated, and the people of their town asked the Baal Shem Tov not to help them, and he did not help.

## 174 — The Bear and the Judge

Once, on the Sabbath before Passover, the Baal Shem Tov prayed the morning prayer. After the prayer he took off his tallit and went outside, and they thought he needed to relieve himself. When he was in the synagogue courtyard, he saw that they were leading a bear, and children were running after it, as they do. And he too walked after the bear. Afterward they led the bear from the gate of the synagogue courtyard to the market, and he too walked after it, and the people were greatly amazed. They led the bear as far as the house of the judge, and then the bear tore the rope, and behaved like a madman. And all the people who had been walking after it fled in fear that it would run through the whole town and harm people.

The Baal Shem Tov stood in his place, and the bear ran into the judge's courtyard, and went into his passageway, and forced open the door of the house. And at once the Baal Shem Tov stood by the window, to see what the bear would do, and some people too, and most of the crowd, stood by the windows to see what the bear would do. At once the bear moved the table from its place, and lifted up the floor, and dug in the earth, until it found a dead baby, a child born of sin, buried under the earth. And they took it in both hands and raised it up before everyone's eyes. The Baal Shem Tov cried out: "You are witnesses!" And in Polish: "Protest!" For the judge had wanted to make a false accusation against the Jews. And the Baal Shem Tov went to the synagogue to finish the prayer. <!-- MATURE: a dead illegitimate infant buried under a floor, kept for a blood libel -->

**Summary:** On the Sabbath before Passover the Baal Shem Tov went out in the middle of the prayer, and walked after a bear that was being led through the streets. In front of the judge's house the bear tore its rope, ran into the house, moved the table and dug under the floor, and found there a dead baby that the judge had hidden. The Baal Shem Tov called on all the people who saw it to be witnesses, for the judge had planned to use the body for a blood libel against the Jews, and went back to finish his prayer.

## 175 — The Horse That Served the Debtor

Once, on a journey, the Baal Shem Tov stayed in a village with one of his people, and he made a great feast in his honor. Meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov spoke with him about his affairs, as people do: whether he had a living, and how he was doing. And he told him about his good situation, all in detail. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Do you have good horses? Let us go and see your horses." They went to the stable, and one small horse pleased him. The Baal Shem Tov asked the master of the house to give him this horse as a gift. He answered him: "Sir, do not ask me for this horse, for it is precious and very dear to me. For at every time and in every hard place, where three good horses cannot pull the wagon, it pulls alone. And several times this has happened to me, and so love for the horse is fixed in my heart."

An hour later they spoke again about his good situation, and how he also had a lot of money that people owed him. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Please, show me your promissory notes." And he showed him all the notes. When he saw one note, the Baal Shem Tov asked him to give him that note as a gift. He answered him: "What does the Rebbe want with this note? The debtor has been dead for several years, and left nothing behind him that can be collected." The Baal Shem Tov said: "Even so, I want it." And he gave him the note as a gift. The Baal Shem Tov took the note, and tore it in two, and they forgave the dead man with a complete forgiveness. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov said: "Please go and see how the horse is." He went, and looked, and there was the horse lying dead, and he understood that it was no empty thing. And the Baal Shem Tov said: "The man who owed you, and had nothing to pay you with, was sentenced to give you satisfaction and work for you in return for the money, and he was reincarnated in the horse. And so he gave you satisfaction, and did hard work. And when his debt was forgiven, he went back to his place, and the horse's life stopped, and so it died completely."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov asked a man for a small, strong horse as a gift that was very dear to him, and the man refused. Afterward he asked for an old promissory note of a man who had died leaving nothing, tore it up, and the man forgave the debtor completely. At once the horse died, and the Baal Shem Tov explained that the dead debtor had been reincarnated in the horse in order to work for the man he owed, and when the debt was forgiven, he went up to his place.

## 176 — The Grave of Moshe, Servant of God

I heard from the rabbi of our community: once the Baal Shem Tov was in Polonne, and traveled from there to the town of Ir Chadash, and the road passes by the cemetery of Ir Chadash. The Baal Shem Tov saw a great pillar of fire standing over a grave. He sent the servant to see on the gravestone who was buried there, and it was written that a tzaddik was buried there, who was called Moshe, servant of God. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Beside him a man will be buried named Rabbi David." There was a man in their town who was called Rabbi David Tseres, and he was very frightened when he heard this, for he thought the Baal Shem Tov was hinting that he would die. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Do not be afraid, you are not the David I spoke of."

About twelve years after the Baal Shem Tov's passing, Rabbi David Purkes came to Polonne for the Sabbath of Chazon. He stopped with his wagon in front of the Rabbi of Polonne, and met with him, and received permission from him to spend the Sabbath in Ir Chadash. He came to the house of the rabbi of Ir Chadash, and did not find him at home, for he had gone on a journey, and he spent the Sabbath there. And Rabbi David wondered at himself the whole Sabbath day, and said: "I do not know what I am doing here. The rabbi, the preacher, is not at home, and I came only to greet the rabbi, for he is a tzaddik." At the end of the Sabbath, which was Tisha B'Av, he grew weak. He called the burial society, and made a will, and lay down on his bed, and passed from the world on Sunday. And they buried him beside the grave of Rabbi Moshe. When they were at the cemetery, they saw what was written on the gravestone, and were amazed, and told this wonder all through the town. Then the mother of the Mokhiach of Polonne, who was very old, remembered the words of the Baal Shem Tov, who had said more than twenty years before that beside this grave Rabbi David would be buried. And they saw that it had been the word of God.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov saw a pillar of fire over the grave of a tzaddik named Moshe, servant of God, in Ir Chadash, and said that beside him a man named Rabbi David would be buried. Twelve years after the Baal Shem Tov's passing Rabbi David Purkes came to spend the Sabbath in Ir Chadash without knowing why he had come, grew weak at the end of the Sabbath and passed away, and was buried exactly beside that grave. The Mokhiach's old mother remembered the prophecy, and everyone saw that it had come true.

## 177 — The Handsome Man Who Was a Brand Plucked from the Fire

Once the Baal Shem Tov went with Rabbi David Purkes the rabbi to the bathhouse. On the way a handsome man came toward them, with well-kept curls, and in fine clothes. When the man came near them, the Baal Shem Tov jumped aside, very far away. When the man had passed before them, the Baal Shem Tov came back, and passed his hands over Rabbi David's eyes, and said: "See who it is who passed before us." And Rabbi David saw that he was "a brand plucked from the fire": the fire had eaten him on all sides.

**Summary:** On the way to the bathhouse the Baal Shem Tov kept away from a handsome, well-groomed man who passed them. When he passed his hands over the eyes of Rabbi David Purkes, Rabbi David saw that the man was like a burnt log, which the fire had eaten on all sides.

## 178 — The Pipe

Once the Baal Shem Tov was traveling, and on the way he smoked his pipe. The stem of the pipe was very long, so that the pipe stuck out of the wagon. As they drove like that, a nobleman and two soldiers riding on horses came toward them, and took the pipe from him, and went on their way. And the Baal Shem Tov too went on traveling. After an hour the Baal Shem Tov stopped, and said to his servant: "Take a horse, and ride on it, to catch up with the soldiers, and take the pipe from them." And so he did. When he caught up with them, he saw that they were sitting on their horses asleep. He took the pipe, and went on his way.

**Summary:** A nobleman and two soldiers snatched the Baal Shem Tov's pipe from him on the road. After an hour the Baal Shem Tov sent his servant to ride after them, and the servant found them asleep on their horses, and took the pipe back.

## 179 — Yom Kippur in the Field

Once a man traveled to the Baal Shem Tov for Yom Kippur. On the day before the eve of Yom Kippur the man traveled all day and all night, so as to hurry and get there. He arrived at daybreak on the eve of Yom Kippur, a parsah from the town of Mezhibuzh. He said in his heart: "Here, I am near the town, and the horse is very tired, for it traveled all night. I will stop here to pray, and meanwhile the horse will graze." After the prayer sleep overcame him. He said in his heart: "What harm if I sleep here two hours? The horse will rest from its weariness, and afterward I will drive fast, and in any case I will surely reach the town before midday." He lay down in the wagon, and fell into a very deep sleep. And when he woke from his sleep, he saw that it was already twilight of Yom Kippur. He was very, very grieved: after all the efforts he had made to travel to the Baal Shem Tov for Yom Kippur, he had to spend Yom Kippur in the field, alone, near the town, because of his own carelessness. And he cried out and wept in the bitterness of his heart all night and all day. At the end of Yom Kippur he drove quickly to the Baal Shem Tov, in the bitterness of his soul. When he came to the Baal Shem Tov, the Baal Shem Tov laughed at him. And the matter was this: the man was meant to raise up with his prayer the prayer of the people in the fields, and Heaven had caused him to be there for this.

**Summary:** A man who traveled all night to be with the Baal Shem Tov for Yom Kippur fell asleep in a field near the town, and woke when Yom Kippur had already begun, and had to pray alone in the field, weeping all day. When he afterward came to the Baal Shem Tov, the Baal Shem Tov laughed, for Heaven had caused him to be there so that he would raise up with his prayer the prayers of the simple people in the fields.

## 180 — The Swimmers in the River

Once the Baal Shem Tov was sitting at a meal with his people, and in the middle of the meal he raised his hands, and made many movements, like a man swimming in a river, and said: "Fool! Do this and this, and you will be saved!" And the thing was a wonder in the eyes of those who saw it. After about an hour a man came, and told how he had fallen with his wagon into the river, and he did not know how to swim. "And at once it came into my mind: I will try to do this and this with my hands. And blessed be God, it helped me, and I got out of the river."

And so too, once, at a meal, the Baal Shem Tov cried out and said: "Fool! You saw some gentiles in the field. Take your bagel, and throw it before their eyes, and they will come to you!" And this time too, after an hour, a man came, and said that he had fallen into the river, and there was no one to save him, for the gentiles were on the hill, and he was in the stream, and they did not see him. "And I remembered that I had a bagel, and I threw the bagel toward the gentiles, and at once they came and saved me, with God's help."

**Summary:** Twice, in the middle of a meal, the Baal Shem Tov shouted instructions to people who were not there: once he made swimming movements and said "Do this and you will be saved," and once he said to throw a bagel toward the gentiles in the field. Each time, an hour later, a man came who told that he had fallen into a river, and that exactly what the Baal Shem Tov had said came into his mind, and so he was saved.

## 181 — The Council of Four Lands

Once, at the time of the assembly of the "Council of Four Lands," the chief parnas was the famous rich rabbi, Rabbi Avraham Abba. He brought the matter before everyone: "See, the name of the Baal Shem Tov has gone out, and from what one hears he is an ignoramus. And how can it be that he has the holy spirit? An ignorant man cannot be pious." They sent from the assembly to the Baal Shem Tov to come to them at once, and so he did. When he came before them, Rabbi Avraham Abba was the chief speaker. He said to the Baal Shem Tov: "From the way you conduct yourself, it seems that the holy spirit rests on you. And some say that your honor is an ignoramus. So let us hear from your mouth whether your honor knows some law."

And the story took place on Rosh Chodesh. He asked him: "Whoever forgot 'Ya'aleh VeYavo,' what is the law for him?" The Baal Shem Tov answered: "This law is not needed either by your honor or by me. For your honor, even if he goes back and prays again, will forget again." (For truly so it was, that he forgot "Ya'aleh VeYavo" the second time too.) "And I certainly will not forget." When he told him the truth, Rabbi Avraham Abba was very frightened that he knew what was hidden. At once he made up his mind to go ahead of the Baal Shem Tov on his way, to the place where they would stop, so as to see his conduct. He hid in a room, and saw through a hole what the Baal Shem Tov was doing. And when he saw that the Baal Shem Tov was about to leave, he drove on from there, to the place where the Baal Shem Tov would stay the night, and hid in secret.

When the Baal Shem Tov came to the inn, they prepared a fine bed for him. When the Baal Shem Tov came and saw the bed, he cried out: "Woe for this bed! They sinned on it with a gentile woman, and how can I sleep on it?" At once the master of the house came, and told him with a great complaint about his son-in-law, how he had sinned with a gentile woman on this bed. At once the Baal Shem Tov said: "Rabbi Abba, did you hear what he is saying?" And at once Rabbi Abba came out and came before him. <!-- MATURE: sexual sin committed on a bed -->

**Summary:** At the Council of Four Lands Rabbi Avraham Abba claimed that the Baal Shem Tov was an ignoramus and could not have the holy spirit, and summoned him to be tested. When he asked him what the law is for one who forgot "Ya'aleh VeYavo," the Baal Shem Tov answered that Rabbi Abba himself would forget again even if he repeated the prayer, and so it was. Rabbi Abba followed him in secret, and saw how the Baal Shem Tov sensed at once a bed on which a sin had been committed, and the master of the house confirmed it, and the Baal Shem Tov turned to Rabbi Abba, who was hiding, and asked whether he had heard.

## 182 — The Disciple's Shoes

Once, on Simchat Torah, the members of the holy circle, the Baal Shem Tov's disciples, were rejoicing and dancing in a circle, and the Shechinah was blazing around them. In the middle of the dancing the shoe of the youngest of the circle tore. He was a very poor man, and was very sorry that because of this he would not be able to dance with his companions and rejoice in the joy of the mitzvah. The Baal Shem Tov's daughter, the righteous Adel, was also standing in the house, at the side, watching their joy. She said to that disciple: "If you promise me that I will give birth to a son this year, I will give you good shoes at once." For she had shoes in her shop. And he promised her that a son would surely be born to her. And so it was: the rabbi, Rabbi Baruch of Tulchin, was born to her.

**Summary:** On Simchat Torah the shoe of the Baal Shem Tov's youngest and poorest disciple tore, and he was sorry that he would not be able to dance. Adel, the Baal Shem Tov's daughter, offered him good shoes from her shop if he would promise her a son, and he promised. That year her son, Rabbi Baruch of Tulchin, was born to her.

## 183 — The Boy Who Was Meant to Drown

In the town of Old Konstantin there was a man who had no children, and he had been before the Baal Shem Tov several times. Once the Baal Shem Tov promised him that his wife would give birth to a son, and ordered him to let him know when she gave birth. And so it was: his wife gave birth to a son, and he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov to bring him the news of the birth, with great joy. When he came to the Baal Shem Tov and gave him the news, the Baal Shem Tov held himself back a long time, and began to weep bitterly, and his hands grew very weak. The man asked: "Why does my master weep?" He said: "I see that on the day the child becomes bar mitzvah, he will drown in the river, God save us." And the Baal Shem Tov said: "Surely you will forget this completely. But this will be your sign: on that day he will put both stockings on one foot, and look for the other stocking. If you then guard him all day with excellent care, so that he does not see the face of the water, the sentence will be canceled. And if you take your eyes off him, he will surely drown in the river, God save us."

In the course of time the whole story was completely forgotten by him. On the day the boy became bar mitzvah, the man went to pray in the synagogue, and the boy was still asleep. When he came back from the synagogue, the boy was searching for something from corner to corner. His father asked him: "What are you looking for?" He said: "One of my stockings is lost." And his father saw that he was wearing both stockings on one foot. When he saw this, he remembered the story, and set his heart on guarding the child on that day. That day the sun was very strong, and all the people of the town ran to the river to bathe. The boy too slipped away and ran to the river, and at once they ran after him, and brought him back home. When his father saw that the boy was pressing very hard to bathe in the river, and it was impossible to guard him, he put him in a room and locked it. All day the boy cried out bitterly: "Let me out! Where is your pity? 'As a father has compassion on his children'! For the sun is burning me!" And they paid no attention to him, and did not give him even a little water to drink.

After the afternoon prayer, when all the people of the town were at the river, there rose out of the water the shape of a head with two hands, and it struck the water with its hand, and said: "This one of mine is not here!" And at once it sank and was not seen. And at once the boy was relieved of his heat, and fell asleep. And with God's help he grew up well, for long days, as the Baal Shem Tov had said.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov promised a childless man a son, and when the son was born he wept, for he saw that the son would drown in the river on the day of his bar mitzvah. He gave the father a sign: on that day the boy would put both stockings on one foot. When the day came and the sign appeared, the father remembered, and locked his son in a room all through the hot day, despite his weeping, and after the afternoon prayer a shape rose from the water that said "This one of mine is not here" and sank, and the boy was saved and grew up well.

## 184 — The Merchant and Jonah

We heard in the name of the holy Rabbi Menachem Nachum of Chernobyl: in a certain community near Mezhibuzh there was a great and very rich man, who was a merchant in Leipzig, and he had had no children for several years, and he did not want in any way to travel to the Baal Shem Tov. Once he traveled to Mezhibuzh on some business, and stayed there with a man who was a very faithful friend of his. They asked each other how they were, and he told him all his affairs. The man said to him: "The whole world travels to the Baal Shem Tov. And why are you too lazy to go to him? Perhaps it will be a time of favor, and you will be remembered with children." And he could not refuse him, and the two of them went together to the Baal Shem Tov.

The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "My advice is: leave your trade in Leipzig, and sell all the goods and the wagons, and lease the *arenda* from your lord. And within this year your wife will give birth to a son, in a good hour. And when she gives birth, let me know." His heart sank, for he did not believe him, and especially since this lord was a hater of Israel, who for fifteen years had not wanted any Jew to live in his villages. When he came home, he did not want to go to the lord, for he thought that surely these were empty words. Two days later the lord himself sent for him, and gave him the arenda, as the Baal Shem Tov had said. And he thought: "Perhaps the lord badly needs money today, and that pressed him to change his custom." And within the year his wife gave birth to a son. But it did not come into his mind to let the Baal Shem Tov know, for he said: "It is chance."

The child grew, and grew and grew, and became a real wild man: "his hand against everyone and everyone's hand against him," and it was impossible to bring him to school. The father truly despaired of his life, and very much regretted having longed for children at the beginning, for it would have been better for him to have no children at all, for truly "better his death than his life." In the course of time it happened that he traveled to Mezhibuzh again, and stayed with that same friend, and told him of the trouble of his heart. He said to him: "You did a foolish thing in not letting the Baal Shem Tov know at once when your wife gave birth, as you were commanded by his holy mouth. But even so, let us go to him now, and ask his advice." When they came before him, the man began to excuse himself before him for not having let him know then. The Baal Shem Tov said: "My advice is: give up the arenda, and sell all the grain and all the things for cash, and travel to Leipzig, and stay on the road for a year. And when you come home, you will find your son turned into truly another man, and he will be great in Torah."

He did as he said, and sold all he had for cash, and traveled to Leipzig. He did his work slowly, and bought little by little, until he had bought all his goods well. All the merchants traveled each to his own place, and he alone remained. He said in his heart: "What more will I do here alone? And the money too has run out." And although only four months had passed, he put all the goods in the wagon, and set out on his way with his wagon driver.

As they traveled, on Friday, the Sabbath eve, they lost their way, and drove all day, and found no place to stay the night. Toward evening they agreed that the wagon would stand on the road, and that one would turn right and the other left, to search; perhaps they would find a resting place that would be good for them. When the merchant had gone a hundred cubits or more from the road, he saw a small house standing in the forest, with candles burning in it. He held himself together and went there. When he went in, he saw one man standing and praying, and one man standing and serving. Since he had taken nothing with him from the wagon, the servant went at once and brought him a shirt and clothes for the Sabbath. He too stood and prayed. After the welcoming of the Sabbath and the evening prayer, the master of the house said "Shabbata tava" with great joy, and they brought them wine to make Kiddush, and delicacies fit for kings, and nothing was lacking. Afterward they brought him a fine bed. When the master of the house saw that the guest hesitated to sleep in the bed, he said: "There is no woman here with us." And indeed he saw no woman there, only the master of the house and the servant. They rose early in the morning, and prayed the morning prayer and Musaf properly, and ate and drank and rejoiced, and took a midday nap. And since the merchant had drunk much wine, he slept until the afternoon prayer. They prayed the afternoon prayer, and ate the third Sabbath meal, and rejoiced until evening, and prayed the evening prayer, and made Havdalah.

After Havdalah the master of the house greeted the guest (for on the Sabbath one does not say "Shalom" in the usual way). The guest was very worried, for he did not know what had become of the driver and his goods. The master of the house said to him: "If you like, sell me your goods in this way: I will pay half the goods in cash, and half on credit until the Leipzig fair, and there I will pay." Although it was very hard for him to give credit to a man when he did not know who he was, even so he felt that he must, and they agreed on the price. After they agreed on the price, he looked out of the window, and saw his wagon standing with the driver and all the goods by the house. He went out to him, and was very angry with him, but he could not change the matter. And he thought in his heart where he would put the goods, for there was no building there except the house alone. The master of the house said: "Give me the goods." And they began to unload the goods, and opened a door to a cellar, and put all the goods there. They made a reckoning between them, and it came to a large sum, and the master of the house paid him half in cash, about eighty thousand zlotys, and for the other half he gave him a note.

As they drove away from there, the merchant said in his heart: "The Baal Shem Tov ordered me to stay a year on the road. And besides, I have sold all the goods. What will I do at home? Better for me to travel to Leipzig again, and buy little by little all the goods I will need." On the way he took his note, and saw that it was signed "Nasyona." He began to investigate and inquire after him, and no one knew him. In Leipzig too, after he had stayed the whole time of the fair and bought all the goods, he began to investigate after the man, and he became a laughingstock in the eyes of everyone who saw him: how does one give credit for such a large sum to a man whose place and deeds one does not know? When all the merchants began to go back each to his own place, he too put all his goods in the wagons, and set out on his way.

When he came to the city gate, he saw the man standing before him, and was frightened. The man said to him: "Your honor has a note of mine for a sum of eighty thousand zlotys. Give me the note, and I will pay you in cash." He asked him: "Tell me, please, who are you?" He answered him: "What do you need to know who I am? I am paying in cash." Even so, he wanted very much to find out the truth. The man said to him: "If you give me my note, I will pay you. And if not, I certainly will not pay. And do as you wish." He gave him the note, and the man paid him the money to the last penny. Afterward he said to him: "Give my regards to the Baal Shem Tov." He asked him: "How shall I tell him in your name?" He said to him: "Tell him: 'Jonah sends you his regards.'"

He went home with joy and a glad heart, and found his son turned into another man, and diligent in his study. And at once he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov, and told him everything that had happened. The Baal Shem Tov asked about Jonah, how he was, and he said: "He commanded me to give my master his regards." The Baal Shem Tov said: "Know that it was Jonah son of Amittai." Thus far.

**Summary:** A rich, childless merchant who did not want to go to the Baal Shem Tov had a son born to him after he followed his advice, but did not let him know, and the son grew up wild. When he came back to the Baal Shem Tov, he was sent to travel for a year to Leipzig, and on the way, on the Sabbath eve, he lost his way and came to a house in the forest, and there spent the Sabbath with a mysterious man who bought all his goods, and paid half in cash and half with a note. At the end of the Leipzig fair the man waited for him at the gate and paid him everything, and sent regards to the Baal Shem Tov in the name of "Jonah," and at home the merchant found his son diligent in Torah, and the Baal Shem Tov revealed to him that the man had been Jonah son of Amittai, the prophet.

## 185 — "What Is My Opponent Doing?"

In a certain community there were three brothers, great scholars and God-fearing. Two of them were faithful to the Baal Shem Tov, and the third was opposed to him. Every time the Baal Shem Tov was in that town, his lodging was with the two brothers, and every time he would ask after the third brother. Once he came to the town on Thursday, and before he went to his lodging, he asked the people of the town after the third brother, like this: "What is my opponent doing?" The people thought they would bring him good news, and said that he was lying on his sickbed, and had not even the power of speech. The Baal Shem Tov said: "We must go to him to visit the sick man."

On Friday the Baal Shem Tov asked the two brothers whether it would be possible to pray on the Sabbath at their brother's. They said: "If he were well, he certainly would not let the Rebbe pray at his house. But now he lies like a stone that no one turns over. What do we care? Surely it is possible, for there is a special room there for praying." They ordered a holy ark and a Torah scroll brought there, and the Baal Shem Tov prayed there on Friday the afternoon and evening prayers, and in the morning he prayed the morning prayer, and drew out the prayer until two hours after midday.
