Before the Torah reading he went out into the passageway to cool off, and at once he heard a sound of wailing, that the sick man was dying. He said to his people: "Let us go and visit the sick man."

The Baal Shem Tov sat by the sick man, and his mother said to him: "Why do you not greet the rabbi?" And he did not answer her at all. She put his hand into the rabbi's hand. The Baal Shem Tov asked the sick man whether he had studied Gemara, and he was silent. And so he asked him several times, and he was silent, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him: "Where are your manners? I ask you, and you answer me nothing!" Then he answered him: "I studied Gemara." He asked him: "In which tractate is the saying 'Are sufferings dear to you?' written?" He said to him: "In Tractate Berakhot." "And what is said there?" He said to him: "'Neither they nor their reward,' etc." The Baal Shem Tov asked him: "Are sufferings dear to you?" And he answered: "Neither they nor their reward." The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Give me your hand!" He gave him his hand, and the Baal Shem Tov raised him up (as in the Gemara). Afterward he told him to put his feet down from the bed, and he did so. Afterward he told him to dress little by little and wash his hands, and he did everything as he told him. Afterward he went with him to the prayer room, and he prayed Musaf with everyone, and recovered, with God's help.

**Summary:** One brother, an opponent of the Baal Shem Tov, lay dying and without speech, and the Baal Shem Tov asked to pray on the Sabbath in his house. When the sick man was dying, the Baal Shem Tov went in to him and demanded that he answer, and asked him the Gemara's question "Are sufferings dear to you?", and the sick man answered as in the Gemara, "Neither they nor their reward." As in the story in the Gemara, the Baal Shem Tov said to him "Give me your hand" and raised him up, and the sick man got up, dressed, and prayed Musaf with everyone.

## 186 — Rabbi Nachman, Who Did Not Hear His Own Praises

Once the Baal Shem Tov was telling the praises of Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka the rabbi, in front of him, while Rabbi Nachman sat far from him. Then Rabbi Nachman came closer, and bent his ear to listen. The thing was a wonder in the eyes of the people, and they asked the Baal Shem Tov, and he said to them: "Rabbi Nachman asked God to give him the gift of hearing only what he needs to hear. And what I tell in his praise, he does not hear. And when he saw that my lips were moving and my voice was not heard, he thought I was saying words of Torah, and so he came closer to hear from near by. And the proof of it: when I say words of Torah, he hears even from far away."

And in another version I heard that the Baal Shem Tov did it on purpose. For when he told his praises, Rabbi Nachman saw that his lips were moving, and his voice was not heard, and when he came closer to listen, the thing was a laughingstock in everyone's eyes. And the Baal Shem Tov did this because he did not like it that Rabbi Nachman had asked God to hear only what he needed, and he said: "Either way: if he is worthy of it by his deeds, the Holy One, blessed be He, will not withhold his reward, as we find with Abraham our father, that after the circumcision five organs were given into his control, and the power of hearing is one of them. And if he is not worthy of it by his deeds, why should he rise in levels?"

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka asked God that he hear only what he needs to hear, and so when the Baal Shem Tov told his praises, he did not hear, and came closer to listen because he thought the Baal Shem Tov was saying Torah. According to one version, the Baal Shem Tov explained Rabbi Nachman's gift by this. According to another version, he did it on purpose, and Rabbi Nachman became a laughingstock in everyone's eyes, for the Baal Shem Tov did not like the request: if Rabbi Nachman is worthy of such a level, God will give it to him even without his asking.

## 187 — The Man the Baal Shem Tov Turned His Face From

A certain man was one of those close to the Baal Shem Tov, and lived in a village, and the Baal Shem Tov always drew him close in all kinds of ways, and on the Days of Awe his lodging was in the Baal Shem Tov's house. Once he came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov turned his face away from him. The man was amazed, and thought in his mind: "Perhaps his thought is roaming in great matters." And since he was used to being with him, he went in to him again after an hour, and the Baal Shem Tov turned his face away from him again. And so he did three times, and the man's heart sank very low, for surely this was no empty thing. And he saw that it was something new, for the Baal Shem Tov used to draw him close in all kinds of ways. He went to Rabbi Gershon of Kitov, and told him everything. Rabbi Gershon went to his brother-in-law, the Baal Shem Tov, and said to him: "What is this, that you push him away, though there is no wrong in his hand, and all his deeds are proper?" He answered him: "This, that you want to bring me into the affairs of the world, to draw people close and speak with them, I do not want."

When the man saw that he had no remedy, he came to Rabbi Gershon, and cried out with a bitter and broken heart: "I understand that this is no empty thing. My life is truly no life, and I cannot bear it in any way." Then Rabbi Gershon went to the Baal Shem Tov a second time, and told him that it was truly a matter of life and death, that he must reveal to him what this was, and what it was for. Then the Baal Shem Tov answered him: "On his forehead is written the sin of a married woman." The man claimed that it was not true, for he had been separated from his wife for more than sixteen years, and how could such a thing be? Then Rabbi Gershon told the Baal Shem Tov that it was on him to investigate the matter, "for I see nothing; only you alone see, and surely the sin is not in the physical, but in subtle matters." And the Baal Shem Tov said that on Friday, at the afternoon prayer, when he has an ascent of the soul to the upper palaces, he would investigate the matter.

In his ascent of the soul he went up to the palace of the Tosafists, and found there no mark of any sin that the man had committed. Afterward he went up higher, to the palace of Maimonides, and there he found written the mark of the sin on that man. And the matter was this: the man had taken upon himself separation from his wife. And according to Maimonides' opinion, this is as if he had said to her "You are to me like my mother," and whoever vows to have no benefit from his wife may not benefit from her jewelry. And the man had needed money to provide for a poor bride, and took his wife's jewelry and pawned it. So it turned out that he had benefited from her and broken his vow, and according to Maimonides' opinion this is as if he had sinned with a married woman. The Baal Shem Tov argued with Maimonides (and I also heard that he called the Rif and the Rosh, and they too argued with Maimonides), then Maimonides conceded to them, and the mark was removed from that man. And since then there is no hint of this to be found in Maimonides' book. <!-- MATURE: a sin likened to adultery; a vow of separation from a wife -->

(And the matter is as Rabbi Chaim Vital wrote on the saying "Rabbi Shimon opened": that is, he opened the channel to expound this. For a tzaddik has the power to open new channels, which is not so with Elijah the Prophet, who is of the nature of an angel, and of whom it is said "by the word of Elijah it was decreed." And as was said in the name of the Baal Shem Tov on the saying "The whole world is fed *bishvil* Chanina my son" (for the sake of Chanina): that is, because of the "path" (*shvil*) that he made. When Rabbi Chanina ben Dosa revealed some secret, a path and a channel was made for the whole world, and it is easy for other people too to understand the thing. And as it is told that the rabbi, the author of the *Beit Yosef*, toiled to understand a certain saying, and when he grasped its truth, he heard it also from several simple people. He asked the Arizal, and the Arizal answered him: "Because you opened the channel, it is easy for everyone to grasp the matter." And this is the meaning of "Had I not lifted the potsherd for you, you would not have found the pearl beneath it." And so, when Maimonides too conceded this ruling, of itself, in this world too the plain meaning of Maimonides' words is understood as the judgment was decided in the upper world.)

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov suddenly turned his face away from a man who had been close to him, and the man suffered until Rabbi Gershon pressed the Baal Shem Tov to reveal to him why. The Baal Shem Tov said that a grave sin was written on his forehead, and the man claimed that he had been separated from his wife for many years, so the Baal Shem Tov investigated in his ascent of the soul. In the palace of Maimonides it came out that according to Maimonides' method, the separation the man had taken on forbade him to benefit from his wife's jewelry, and he had pawned it to provide for a poor bride. The Baal Shem Tov, together with the Rif and the Rosh, argued with Maimonides until he conceded, and the mark was removed from the man.

## 188 — Rabbi Nachman of Kosov and the Thought

Rabbi Nachman of Kosov the rabbi was an opponent of the Baal Shem Tov. (And I heard that the Baal Shem Tov was of the soul of King David, and Rabbi Nachman of the soul of King Saul, and so he was David's enemy all his days.) Once the Baal Shem Tov said: "Rabbi Nachman is pursuing me to kill me (as is known, there is a mystical intention in the falling on the face to kill an enemy), but with God's help he will not catch me."

Once the disciples came to their master, Rabbi Nachman, and said to him: "What is this, that the whole world travels to the Baal Shem Tov, and praises him greatly? Why do you not meet with him, and examine his jug, and we will know with whom the truth lies? Why should this be a stumbling block for us?" Rabbi Nachman accepted their words, and traveled to Mezhibuzh to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov received him with great honor. Afterward the two of them went into a special room, and sent everyone out of there, except one man who hid in a certain place. Rabbi Nachman said: "Israel, is it true that you say you know people's thoughts?" He said to him: "Yes." He said to him: "If so, do you know what I am thinking now?"

The Baal Shem Tov answered: "It is known that thought does not rest, and roams over many things, putting off one form and putting on another. If you fix your thought on one thing, then I will know." And Rabbi Nachman did so. The Baal Shem Tov said: "The four-letter name of God is in your thought." Rabbi Nachman said: "That you can understand by yourself. This thought I must think always, as it is written 'I have set the Lord before me always.' And so, when I leave all thoughts and fix my thought on one thing, the four-letter name must be before my eyes." The Baal Shem Tov said: "But there are several holy names, and you could have concentrated on whichever of them you wanted." Then Rabbi Nachman admitted that the Baal Shem Tov was right. And afterward they spoke of the secrets of the Torah.

**Summary:** Rabbi Nachman of Kosov, who was an opponent of the Baal Shem Tov, came to test him at his disciples' request, and asked whether he knew what he was thinking. The Baal Shem Tov asked him to fix his thought on one thing, and said that he was thinking of the four-letter name of God. When Rabbi Nachman claimed that this was obvious, the Baal Shem Tov answered that there are many holy names, and even so he had known exactly which one, and Rabbi Nachman admitted it, and the two spoke of the secrets of the Torah.

## 189 — The Fish from the Pond

Once the Baal Shem Tov saw that Rabbi Nachman was setting out on a journey, and the Baal Shem Tov too traveled after him, so that Rabbi Nachman would reach his lodging for the Sabbath first, and the Baal Shem Tov would come after him and be his guest, and Rabbi Nachman would have no fish for the Sabbath, and be put to shame. When Rabbi Nachman saw that the Baal Shem Tov was pursuing him, he twisted his route along crooked ways, and traveled two days without a road and without any village. And the Baal Shem Tov knew his way, and traveled after him too.

On Thursday toward evening Rabbi Nachman reached a village, where a Jew lived. As soon as he came there, he asked whether fish could be found here, for on Friday he would not travel on from here. The master of the house told him that it was quite impossible, for the place was about twenty parsahs or more from rivers on every side. Rabbi Nachman asked him: "How can a Jew be without water for drinking, for washing the hands and for immersion?" He answered him that he had two ponds of water: one of clear water for drinking, and one of muddy water for washing and immersion. He said: "If so, I will surely get fish." And at once he said to his servant: "Look, I once saw a fisherman who took a stick, and tied a string to it, and threw it into the river, and caught fish with it. So take a stick and a string, and go at once to the pond, and do the same." He did so, and pulled a big fish out of the pond.

The next day, when Rabbi Nachman saw that the Baal Shem Tov would soon arrive, he went out joyfully to meet him and welcome him, and greeted him, and said to him: "With God's help, I have fish for the Sabbath." The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "Many thanks to you for sending *duntses* against me, and I had to busy myself with them. And because of that you got fish. And had it not been for that, you would certainly not have got fish in any way."

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov pursued Rabbi Nachman of Kosov, so as to arrive after him for the Sabbath and shame him by his having no fish for his guest. Rabbi Nachman fled by crooked ways to a village far from any river, and even so his servant caught a big fish from a pond. When Rabbi Nachman told him joyfully that he had fish, the Baal Shem Tov answered him that he had got them only because he had sent *duntses* against him, and the Baal Shem Tov had had to busy himself with them.

## 190 — The Parable of the Crown

Once Rabbi Nachman (of Kosov) heard some of our fellow Hasidim speaking ill of the Baal Shem Tov, and he was very angry about it. He told them a parable: "Once two great ministers made a crown for the king, and spent a great fortune on it. When they came to finish the crown, a dispute fell between them, how to set the precious stone: this one said this way, and that one said that way. And both of them intended the greatness of the king. When a quarrel arose between them over it, one of the common people passed by, and spoke as one of them did, and they rebuked him sharply: 'What are you doing here, putting yourself into a quarrel that is not yours? If we quarrel over it, it is because we know very well the greatness of the king, and according to his greatness it must be this way or that way.' And so too the dispute between us is an old dispute, which was between Saul and King David, and afterward between Hillel and Shammai. And how can you stick your heads into it?"

**Summary:** When people among the followers of Rabbi Nachman of Kosov spoke ill of the Baal Shem Tov, Rabbi Nachman was angry with them. He told them a parable of two ministers who quarreled over how to set a stone in the king's crown, both intending the king's honor, and rebuked a simple man who interfered. So too his dispute with the Baal Shem Tov is a lofty dispute, like that between Saul and David and between Hillel and Shammai, and simple people must not interfere in it.

## 191 — The Blind Son in Istanbul

In Istanbul there was a very rich man, and he had a wonderful only son, and suddenly he went blind. They asked the doctors to heal him, and after all the means they tried, they did no good at all, and they gave up on curing him. When the Baal Shem Tov was in that city, he told the man that he would heal his son completely. The man was very glad at his words, and brought him into his house with great honor. When the rich man's wife saw him, she despised him in her heart, for he was not dressed in fine clothes, because of the hardships of the road and of the days. She was angry with her husband and said: "Why did you bring him to me? All the doctors did no good at all, and what good will this one do with names?" And she spoke with contempt of the holy names.

The Baal Shem Tov was angry that she did not believe in the power of the names of God, and said: "Where is the blind child? Bring him to me, and I will heal him, so that he sees at once, like anybody." When he said this, her spirit was alarmed, for one who wants to lie puts his testimony far off, and he was saying that in this very moment the child would see. Perhaps he would make his words come true. At once they brought the sick boy to him. He whispered something in his ears, and at once he ordered a Gemara to be brought before him, and ordered him to read. And the child read, like anybody. There was great joy among the household. And at once the Baal Shem Tov passed his hand over his eyes, and the child became as before, blind. And there was great weeping in the house.

They pleaded with him, bowing and prostrating themselves, and wanted to give him an enormous sum of money. The Baal Shem Tov answered the wicked woman, who had mocked the holy names: "You may not benefit from them. For from the start I did not want to do this for my own honor, Heaven forbid, or for money, but only for the sanctification of His blessed name. I showed you with your own eyes the great power of the holiness of the name, but you will not benefit from them." And he left them like that.

**Summary:** In Istanbul the Baal Shem Tov promised a rich man to heal his blind son, but the rich man's wife despised him and mocked the power of the holy names. The Baal Shem Tov whispered in the child's ear, and the child at once saw and read from a Gemara, and then the Baal Shem Tov passed his hand over his eyes, and the child became blind again. He refused all money, and told the woman that he had shown her the power of the name, but whoever mocks the holy names will not benefit from them.

## 192 — The Hands of the Woman from Wallachia

In the land of Wallachia there was a man, a famous magnate, and suddenly his wife fell ill in both her hands, and she could not raise them at all. He went with her to all the doctors, and they did no good at all, and gave up on curing her. He asked Tatar healers too, and no cure came for her affliction. And he decided to travel with her from town to town; perhaps a cure for her affliction would come through a Baal Shem or doctors. On his travels he heard from afar of the name of the Baal Shem Tov, and traveled to Mezhibuzh. When they arrived there, they went to the Baal Shem Tov, and he ordered that the woman stay in Mezhibuzh, and she stayed there many days. In the course of time they came to the Baal Shem Tov several times, and he ordered that she stay longer.

After many days the Baal Shem Tov once ordered the wagon and horses made ready, and ordered the man to travel with his sick wife after them. On their journey they came to a village, where one of the Baal Shem Tov's people lived. When they arrived there, the Baal Shem Tov asked the tollkeeper whether he would give him lodging. The tollkeeper was greatly amazed at this question, for truly it was a great privilege for him that the Baal Shem Tov should stay with him. The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "When I stay with you, the windows, the gates and the doors will be shut, and they will not be opened to anyone, whoever he may be, even a great nobleman. And if he presses very hard, answer him: 'The rabbi, the Baal Shem Tov, is here.'" And the tollkeeper promised to do so. They stopped with him at the tavern, and at once did as the Baal Shem Tov had said, and got ready for the evening meal. After the meal they all slept, except the Baal Shem Tov, who sat at the table and studied some book, and the sick woman sat at the back of the house.

That day the brother of the nobleman who owned the tavern came to him, and they had not seen each other for more than twelve years. There was great joy between them, and they ate and drank and got drunk. In the middle of their talk the nobleman began to boast how he had built a very good and splendid tavern in the well-known place, and praised it highly. The guest asked to be given a horse, so as to ride there. They gave him a good, choice horse, and since the place was near the castle, he rode in one garment only. As soon as he set out, a storm wind with snow began, and he was held up on the way for several hours, until he reached the tavern. He knocked on the door for them to open for him, and they answered him that it was impossible to open for him, for the rabbi, the Baal Shem Tov, was here. He asked several times, with great pleading, that they open for him, for he was frozen through, and they answered him the same answer. Until the last time the Baal Shem Tov signaled that they should open for him.

When the nobleman came into the house, he walked back and forth, until he had warmed up a little. And when he had recovered, he asked: "Who is this Baal Shem Tov?" They showed him the Baal Shem Tov, and at once the fire of anger began to burn in him. And he walked back and forth, and asked again: "Who is he, and where is this Baal Shem Tov, because of whom they did not open the door for me, until I truly could have died from the great cold?" And they showed him the Baal Shem Tov. Until the third time the fire of anger burned in him completely, and when they showed him the Baal Shem Tov, he drew his sword from its sheath, and raised his hand against the Baal Shem Tov to kill him. At once the Baal Shem Tov cried out to the sick woman: "Raise both your hands!" And she did so. And at once an exchange was made: the woman's hands were restored to health as before, and the nobleman could not bring his hand back, and his hands withered. He pleaded greatly before the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov said: "I cannot help you, for the exchange has already been made, and it can no longer be changed."

**Summary:** A rich woman from Wallachia, whose hands were paralyzed, waited a long time in Mezhibuzh by the Baal Shem Tov's instruction, until one day he took her with him to a village tavern, and ordered that the door not be opened to anyone. At night a nobleman knocked in a storm, and was frozen until they finally opened for him, and in his anger at the Baal Shem Tov he drew a sword to kill him. At that moment the Baal Shem Tov cried out to the woman to raise her hands, and she raised them, and her hands were healed, while the nobleman's hands were paralyzed, and this exchange could no longer be changed.

## 193 — The Son Who Struck His Father

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in a certain community, and his lodging was on the upper floor. Once he saw a man passing in the street, and at once he ordered his servant: "Run quickly, and call that man to me." The boy ran and called him. At first the man's heart was frightened, and he did not want to go, but even so he could not refuse, and went to the Baal Shem Tov. When he came, the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "You committed a transgression today." The man said, as if denying it: "What does your honor care?" The Baal Shem Tov said: "But you committed a great transgression, for you struck your father."

And the story was this: his father was old, and was shut in the house that day. His father went to bring vodka into the house, and stayed there a long time. The man went to see why his father was taking so long, and saw that he was drinking vodka from a big vessel that stood under the barrels. The man was very angry, and struck his father on the head with this vessel. When the Baal Shem Tov told him the truth, at once he wept bitterly, and asked him to give him a penance (a repair) for it. And the Baal Shem Tov gave him a penance: that he fast on Mondays and Thursdays, and also stand in the synagogue in the place of the mourners, and other things besides. And he took upon himself to carry out the order of penance. The Baal Shem Tov warned him that if he changed anything at all, Heaven forbid, he would not complete his year.

During the time he was carrying out the order of penance, his mother began to talk to her son and coax him, and mocked the Baal Shem Tov greatly, until she talked him into it, and he gave up the order of penance. And so it happened to him: he did not complete his year, God save us. And so told the rabbi of Turov, before whom the story took place.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov called from the window to a man who was passing in the street, and told him that he had struck his father that day, and so it was: the man had seen his old father drinking vodka, and struck him on the head with a vessel. The man wept and took upon himself an order of penance, and the Baal Shem Tov warned him that if he stopped, he would not complete his year. His mother mocked the Baal Shem Tov and persuaded him to stop, and he died before the year was out.

## 194 — The Mother Who Had Nothing to Eat

That rabbi also told: once, at the third Sabbath meal, the Baal Shem Tov said words of Torah, and they lingered several hours into the night. He called his servant, and ordered him to take some zlotys from his chest, and go to a certain house, "for a woman has given birth there, and she has nothing to eat, not even the first meal." At once the servant went there with the purse of money, and the door was still shut, for they had not yet cut the cord. And he gave her the money, as the Baal Shem Tov had commanded.

**Summary:** In the middle of a third Sabbath meal that went on long, the Baal Shem Tov sent his servant with money to a certain house, for he knew that a poor woman had given birth there and had nothing to eat. The servant arrived before the cord had been cut, and gave her the money.

## 195 — The Priest on the Eve of Passover

Once the Baal Shem Tov's disciples came to his house on the eve of Passover after midday, to bake the matzot for the mitzvah, as is the custom to bake them in a group and while saying Hallel. And there was the Baal Shem Tov walking back and forth in the synagogue courtyard, deeply absorbed in thought. The disciples waited several hours, and were very sorry about it. Meanwhile they saw the Baal Shem Tov see the priest from afar, and go to him, and begin to speak with him, and walk with him up and down. Afterward he brought him to his house, seated him, and served him Passover mead, and spoke with him a great deal more. Afterward the priest left, and the Baal Shem Tov accompanied him. Afterward he said: "Now, quickly, quickly, let us begin to bake the matzot, for the day is already turning toward evening."

The disciples asked him: "What did you have to talk about with the priest so much?" And he said: "The priest intended to throw a stabbed baby, born of sin, into the street of the synagogue on the night of Passover, and to lay the blame on all the people of the town. And through my speaking with him much and honoring him, it was uprooted from the priest's heart to do it." <!-- MATURE: a planned blood libel with a stabbed infant -->

**Summary:** On the eve of Passover, while the disciples waited for hours to bake matzot, the Baal Shem Tov went walking with the priest, invited him to his house, honored him and spoke with him a great deal. Only when the priest left did he say to hurry and bake. He explained that the priest had planned to throw a dead baby by the synagogue on the night of Passover and accuse the Jews, and thanks to the conversation and the honor he gave up his intention.

## 196 — The Wood Bought in the Middle of the Prayer

Once the Baal Shem Tov was leading the prayers before the ark, and in the middle of the prayer he stopped his prayer, and went out into the street in front of the synagogue. He saw a gentile selling wood, and bought a wagonload of wood from him. The gentile brought the wood after him to the study house, and the Baal Shem Tov ordered that he be paid for the wood, and for bringing the wood to the study house he ordered that he be given vodka. The gentile said: "Blessed is the God of the Jews, who has such a holy people!" For if a gentile had bought from him, he certainly would have given him nothing for that. The disciples asked the Baal Shem Tov why he had stopped in the middle of his prayer to buy wood. He answered that during his prayer he had seen an accusation on high against the Jews who live in the villages, that they cheat the gentiles in their accounts, and he had to stop the accuser's mouth. And by the gentile's praising the Jews greatly, the accuser's claims were stopped.

**Summary:** In the middle of the prayer the Baal Shem Tov went out into the street, bought a wagonload of wood from a gentile, paid him, and also gave him a drink for the hauling. The gentile blessed the God of the Jews for having such a holy people, and the Baal Shem Tov explained that with this he had stopped the mouth of the accuser, who was claiming in Heaven that the village Jews cheat the gentiles.

## 197 — The Speech of Birds and Animals

A story: the rabbi the Mokhiach, Rabbi Aryeh Leib of Polonne, longed very much to learn the wisdom of the speech of animals and birds, and the speech of palm trees, because he needed it for reproof (for his sermons). And since his intention was for the sake of Heaven, he decided to travel to the Baal Shem Tov, and ask him to teach him these wisdoms. And he thought that the Baal Shem Tov would surely do as he wished, since his intention was for the sake of Heaven.

When he came into the Baal Shem Tov's room, and there were many people there, the Baal Shem Tov did not turn to him at all. After he had stood there a long time, the Baal Shem Tov greeted him, in a weak voice and offhandedly. The Mokhiach was astonished at the sight, for he was close to him, and the Baal Shem Tov was fond of him. Even so, out of his great longing to learn these wisdoms, he did not give up, and stayed on with him, for he said: "Perhaps it is chance, and I will find a good moment to come to him."

One day the Baal Shem Tov had a journey to make, from the town of Miropol to Kaminka, and the Baal Shem Tov told the Mokhiach to travel with him. The Mokhiach was filled with joy, for on the road he would surely get what he wanted. They traveled together, with some other people. On the way, at the inn, the Baal Shem Tov went to sleep in a special room in the attic, and the Mokhiach waited until he woke from his sleep. He climbed the ladder to the attic where the Baal Shem Tov was. When he reached the top rung, he saw the Baal Shem Tov sitting and performing unifications, his face burning like torches. He was so frightened that from great fear he almost fell off the rung, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and said to him: "Stand, do not be afraid! What do you want?" And from great fear the Mokhiach was struck silent and did not answer.

When they left the inn, the Baal Shem Tov told the Mokhiach to sit with him in his wagon. He sat with him, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "I know that the whole purpose of your coming to my camp was only to learn the wisdom of the speech of birds, etc. Come, and I will teach you well."

And the opening was this: "It is known that in the Chariot on high there is the face of an ox, the face of a man, the face of an eagle and the face of a lion. The choicest in the Chariot is the face of a man, and from it the life flows to man below. And from the ox above, by way of the chain of levels, cause and effect, and many contractions, the life flows to all the domestic animals below. And from the lion the life flows to all the wild beasts of the field below. And from the eagle, to the birds below. And this is the secret of *Perek Shirah*: according to the speech in the Chariot on high, in each and every living creature, so it comes down lower and lower, in the domestic animals, the wild beasts and the birds. And whoever is wise and understands of his own mind, and looks at each thing in its root above, in the Chariot on high, can know from it all the details of the ways of speech of the domestic animals, the wild beasts and the birds." This is the general rule of the matter.

And in the details of the matter, the Baal Shem Tov told him awesome and wondrous secrets, until the Mokhiach knew the matter thoroughly. And after the Baal Shem Tov had revealed to him the main depth of the secret of this wisdom, he explained to him along the way passages in the Zohar and the *Tikkunim*. And the Mokhiach heard them with one ear, and with the other ear he heard how the birds talk, and how the domestic animals and the wild beasts talk. And all these secrets he explained to him on that journey, until they drew near the town.

When they drew near the town, the Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Have you understood this wisdom well?" He said to him: "Yes." The Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over the Mokhiach's face, and all the details of the secrets of this wisdom were forgotten by him, and nothing was left in his memory but the opening. And the Baal Shem Tov was silent, and said: "If you had needed to know this wisdom, for it to be of use to you in the service of the Creator, I would have hurried of my own accord to teach you. So I taught you this wisdom to quench your thirst, and it has been forgotten by you, for it is not part of your service. 'You shall be wholehearted,' etc."

**Summary:** The Mokhiach of Polonne longed to learn the language of living creatures for his sermons, and at first the Baal Shem Tov ignored him, and then took him with him on a journey. On the way he taught him how all living creatures receive their life and their speech from the four faces of the Chariot on high, until the Mokhiach truly heard the birds and the animals talking. When they reached the town, the Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over his face, and the Mokhiach forgot everything except the opening, and the Baal Shem Tov explained that he had taught him only to quench his thirst, for this wisdom was not part of his service of God.

## 198 — The Night Journey, and the Woman Who Was Saved

Once the Baal Shem Tov sat at the third Sabbath meal, and was deeply absorbed in thought, and did not say Torah at the third meal at all, and the thing was a wonder in the eyes of the disciples. After he had said the blessing of Havdalah, and his custom was every Saturday night to drive out of the town, about half a parsah, and come back to the town, on that Saturday night he said to make ready the wagon and horses, and that some of the disciples should travel with him. He ordered provisions for the road to be prepared, and the chest with all the clothes to be taken, and the thing was a wonder in the eyes of all his household. And he did not say at all where he was going.

When they went out of the town gate, he ordered his disciples to turn their faces toward him, and he also ordered the driver to turn his face toward him, and to tie up the reins, and the horses would go by themselves. And so it was. The disciples saw that they were traveling from town to town: every quarter of an hour they truly entered another town. So they traveled all night, until day broke, and they traveled a very great distance that night, by the shortening of the road. They came to a big city, and the horses stopped by a big, splendid house. The Baal Shem Tov told the driver to drive into the courtyard with the horses. The master of the house got up from his sleep, and greeted them, and asked them: "Where are you from?" They said to him: "From Volhynia." He asked them: "If you are merchants, you should have stayed at the inns meant for that. And if you are preachers of reproof and sermons, you should have stayed at the house of the parnas of the month." The Baal Shem Tov answered him: "What do you care? I will stay only a few hours." And the master of the house went away.

The Baal Shem Tov got down from his wagon, took his pipe, and went into the kitchen to take an ember from there, to light the tobacco. And there a young woman got up from her bed, and came into the kitchen too, dressed in only part of her clothes. The Baal Shem Tov told her to take an ember and put it in his pipe, and she did so. The Baal Shem Tov said to her: "Do you know me?" She said: "No." He said to her: "Why, I am your uncle, and I live in Mezhibuzh. And what are you doing here?" The woman said: "I was the wife of the master of the house's son, and my husband died. And my father-in-law wants me to take his brother's son as husband, and he is still a boy. I cannot oppose him, but it is against my will." The Baal Shem Tov said to her: "Do not worry. I will be like a father to you. If you like, travel with me today to my place, and there I will give you a good man as husband, as you wish." The woman said: "Yes, I will go." He said to her: "If so, go and get ready, for we will not stay here long." The woman went, and took her clothes and her jewelry, and put them in a chest.

When her father-in-law saw what she was doing, he asked her: "Where are you going, that you are getting ready?" She said to him: "This man is my uncle, and he wants to take me to his country, and give me a husband there." The master of the house was angry with the Baal Shem Tov, and ran to him in his fury, and said to him: "What are you doing to me? Taking my daughter-in-law without my knowledge, with all the clothes and the jewelry?" The Baal Shem Tov took him into a closed room, and said to him: "Know that I am the Baal Shem Tov. Yesterday I left my home, and covered all this long way by the shortening of the road. And the matter is this: your daughter-in-law, after her husband died, abandoned herself, and sinned with several noblemen. And in short, not long ago she promised a certain nobleman to convert and marry him.

"And yesterday, at the third Sabbath meal, her grandfather, who was a famous tzaddik and the greatest of his generation, came to me from the upper world, and asked me to repair his granddaughter, this woman, who has a lofty soul, and has fallen into the depths of the *kelipot*, so that she would not remain there, Heaven forbid. And I promised him. And the woman set the time for this day, and surely in two hours the nobleman will come with soldiers, and will take the woman with all the clothes and jewelry by force, and she will convert. So I advise you, and God will be with you, and it will be counted to you as a great mitzvah and as the saving of lives." The words entered the heart of the master of the house, and he believed him. And the Baal Shem Tov promised him that he would return to him all the things the woman had taken that did not belong to her. The Baal Shem Tov took the woman, and brought her back with him. And the disciples, who knew nothing of this, were astonished at the sight. All the way the Baal Shem Tov drew her close, and she called him "my uncle." <!-- MATURE: a widow's immorality with noblemen and a planned conversion to marry one -->

When they reached Mezhibuzh, the Baal Shem Tov matched her with a very respected man, a famous rich man. At the time of the wedding, before the canopy, the woman came to the Baal Shem Tov's room, to confess before him and ask for a penance for her sins, for while she was in the Baal Shem Tov's house, the evil that had been in her was uprooted from her heart. When the Baal Shem Tov saw that she was speaking from the core of her heart, he said to her: "How did it come into your mind that I am your uncle? Know that there never was such a thing. It was only because you had set a time to convert, and your grandfather asked me to save you. And if you do not repent properly, you will surely die at once." She wept very bitterly, and asked him to give her a penance. The Baal Shem Tov answered: "This is your penance: this match will be canceled, for you are not worthy to marry such a man, since you know the bitterness of your soul. Rather, you will marry a baker, and sit in the market with bagels. And all the things your father-in-law gave you, send them back to him, for what is his offense and what is his sin? He gave only on the strength of his faith in me, to save your soul from the pit." And so she did, and accepted the penance with a whole heart, and became a completely righteous woman. Afterward her grandfather came to the Baal Shem Tov in a dream, and said: "May your mind be at rest, as you have set my mind at rest."

**Summary:** On a Saturday night the Baal Shem Tov traveled by night, by the shortening of the road, to a distant city, and told a young widow that he was her uncle, and persuaded her to come with him. To her angry father-in-law he revealed that she had promised to convert and marry a nobleman that very day, and that her righteous grandfather had asked him from Heaven to save her. In Mezhibuzh he matched her with a rich man, but when she came before the canopy to ask for penance with a whole heart, he revealed the truth to her, canceled the match, ordered her to marry a baker and sell bagels in the market and to return all the things to her father-in-law, and she became a righteous woman, and her grandfather came to thank the Baal Shem Tov in a dream.

## 199 — The Priest on the Eve of Yom Kippur

Once, on the eve of Yom Kippur, before Kol Nidrei, the whole congregation gathered in the study house. The Baal Shem Tov stood, and did not begin to pray, and it could be seen on him that he was in great perplexity. They waited a very long time, and all the people burst into tears, for they all understood that this was no empty thing. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov looked out of the window, and saw an old priest walking in front of the study house, and went out to meet him. The Baal Shem Tov began to speak with him and ask how he was, and drew him into conversation, until he went with him to his house. The Baal Shem Tov spoke with him: "Why do you not take a wife? 'He did not create it to be empty'..." The priest answered: "Although your words are right, by their customs a priest may not take a wife." The Baal Shem Tov argued with him much about it, that at least in his old age he should keep the mitzvah of being fruitful and multiplying, and give up his priesthood. The priest said: "To marry a simple woman is not fitting for my honor, and a woman of a decent family will not agree to have me take her." The Baal Shem Tov said: "Look, such-and-such a nobleman has a beautiful daughter, and surely he will agree to give her to you as a wife." And he spoke with him a great deal, until the priest agreed, and her beauty entered his heart, until out of his lust he had a seminal emission.

At once the Baal Shem Tov went to the study house, and they began to pray Kol Nidrei. After the prayer the people close to him came to him, and he told them the story: there had been a great accusation, so that it was impossible for the prayers to rise on high, because this priest was old and had never had a seminal emission. So he had to speak with him until it happened. They asked him: "How did you know that it had happened?" And he said: "Because at once I could not stand near him." And with God's help the mouths of the accusers were stopped. <!-- MATURE: sexual content (an old priest brought to lust and a seminal emission) -->

**Summary:** On the eve of Yom Kippur the Baal Shem Tov stood in perplexity and did not begin Kol Nidrei, and the congregation wept. He went out to an old priest who was passing the study house, and persuaded him to think of marrying a beautiful nobleman's daughter, until the priest became impure. Afterward he explained that this purity of the priest, who had never become impure, had created an accusation that kept the prayers from rising, and that the moment it happened he could not stand near him, and the accusation was stopped.

## 200 — The Shofar Blasts at the Maggid's

I heard that the great Maggid of Mezritch did not blow the shofar himself, but the holy Rabbi Menachem Mendel, who passed away in the Holy Land, would blow, and the Maggid would call out the blasts. And even before his passing, when he was ill in his legs for several years, he would call them out from his room. Once Rabbi Menachem Mendel was not in Mezritch on Rosh Hashanah, and the Maggid commanded the great rabbi Rabbi Levi Yitzchak, head of the rabbinical court of Berditchev, to blow. Rabbi Levi Yitzchak prepared himself for it, and went with awe and fear and with great commotion, as was his way. And as soon as the Maggid called out the first blast before him, he saw great lights, and fainted. The Maggid said: "I do not know what was the matter with him. Mendel sees more, and is not so afraid." And they had to give the blowing to another man, who would not see and not be afraid.

**Summary:** In the study house of the Maggid of Mezritch, Rabbi Menachem Mendel always blew the shofar. When he was away, the Maggid asked Rabbi Levi Yitzchak of Berditchev to blow, and Rabbi Levi Yitzchak saw great lights at the first blast and fainted, and they had to pass the blowing to a man who does not see and is not afraid. The Maggid was surprised, for Rabbi Menachem Mendel sees more and even so is not so afraid.

## 201 — The Arizal and Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai

Once Rabbi David Purkes the rabbi dreamed that he came to the Baal Shem Tov, and saw four men standing beside him. Two of them he knew, and the third was Elijah the Prophet, and the fourth was hidden from him, for he did not know who he was, and was also ashamed to ask the Baal Shem Tov. Once, after the Baal Shem Tov's passing, Rabbi David was with the Maggid, and the Maggid asked him what the man's appearance, form and movements had been like. When Rabbi David told him, the Maggid said: "That is Rabbi Isaac Luria (the Arizal)."

And also, once they heard from the Baal Shem Tov, who said of the great rabbi Rabbi Chaim Tsanzer of Brody that he was a spark of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai. The Maggid said: "Why are you surprised at this? He truly had the very form of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai."

**Summary:** Rabbi David Purkes dreamed that he saw four men beside the Baal Shem Tov, among them Elijah the Prophet and a man he did not know, and after the Baal Shem Tov's passing the Maggid of Mezritch identified the fourth man from his description as the Arizal. The Baal Shem Tov also said that Rabbi Chaim Tsanzer of Brody was a spark of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai, and the Maggid said that even his face was like the face of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai.

## 202 — The Holy Wine of Wallachia

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the land of Wallachia. There they had a "holy wine," which drips from the grapes, and when two or three small drops of it are mixed into a cup, it cannot be drunk because of its great strength. The master of the house honored the Baal Shem Tov with a small cup of this wine. When he tasted it, the Baal Shem Tov said: "Your wine is good; why is your cup small?" The master of the house answered: "Because it is dangerous to drink a big cup." The Baal Shem Tov said: "I am not afraid of that." They gave him a big cup, and he drank all of it. Everyone looked at him, and his face became red, and all his hair stood up like real fire, and everyone was frightened. But the Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over his face, and drove off his wine in a moment. They were greatly amazed at this, and he said: "Our Sages said: 'Wine is strong, fear dispels it.' And when I reflected on His blessed greatness, fear and trembling at His blessed greatness fell on me, and they dispelled the wine completely."

**Summary:** In Wallachia the Baal Shem Tov drank a big cup of "holy wine," so strong that usually only drops of it are drunk. His face turned red and his hair stood up like fire, and everyone was frightened, but he passed his hands over his face, and the wine wore off at once. He explained that he had reflected on God's greatness, and the fear of God had dispelled the wine, as our Sages said.

## 203 — The Rabbi Who Had Doubts About the Tzaddikim

Once the Baal Shem Tov was traveling with his disciples, and they had to spend the night in a forest. The disciples said jokingly: "Let us see what kind of feast the Rebbe will make for us!" The Baal Shem Tov said: "Sleep now. And tomorrow, God willing, I will make you a great feast, with several kinds of delicacies." The next day they came to a big city, and stayed at the house of a rich man. To this rich man had come his uncle, who was a rabbi in a community eighty parsahs from him, and whom he had not seen for more than twelve years. In his honor the rich man made a great feast, and invited all the people of the city. Although they had heard of the name of the Baal Shem Tov, they did not think much of him. After the prayer in the synagogue all the people of the city came to his house, and his uncle the rabbi sat at the head of the guests, and the Baal Shem Tov was still praying at the back of the house. The uncle, the rabbi, asked: "Who is it who is still praying?" The master of the house answered: "That is the Baal Shem Tov." And they did not think anything of him. And his holy disciples too they did not call to recline at the table with them.

When the Baal Shem Tov finished his prayer, he came to the rabbi's place, and said to him: "I have a secret matter to tell you." And he went with him to a hidden place. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Your honor has a certain flaw." The rabbi said: "No." The Baal Shem Tov answered: "Every time you want to concentrate on 'Hear, O Israel,' the shape of a cross stands before you." And the rabbi denied it. The Baal Shem Tov answered: "How can it be a lie? You have fasted eighty fasts over it!" And the rabbi had to admit it, and wept before him to help him. The Baal Shem Tov said to him: "Your cure is that you not have doubts about the great tzaddikim." And the rabbi took upon himself with a whole heart that he would not go back to his foolishness. The Baal Shem Tov said: "Today try to concentrate on this intention, and you will see that since you have repented, you are healed." And so it was. And at once the rabbi took the Baal Shem Tov by the hand, and seated him at the head of the guests, and all his disciples too reclined at the head, and they honored them with great honor.

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov's disciples joked at night in the forest about the feast he would make them, he promised them a great feast the next day. In the city they came to, an important rabbi who looked down on the Baal Shem Tov sat at the head of a great feast, and the Baal Shem Tov revealed to him in secret that every time he tried to concentrate on the Shema, a foreign shape stood before him, although he had fasted eighty fasts over it. The rabbi admitted it, and the Baal Shem Tov said that the repair was not to have doubts about the tzaddikim, and when the rabbi accepted this and saw that he was healed, he seated the Baal Shem Tov and his disciples at the head of the table.

## 204 — The Child Who Came Back to Life at His Circumcision

In Mezhibuzh there was a man who had no children, and he always mentioned himself before the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov promised him that he would surely have children. When he grew old, he troubled the Baal Shem Tov several times, and with God's help his wife bore him a son in her old age. Not many days passed, and the baby died, within the days after the birth. At once the man came to the Baal Shem Tov, in a stir and in anger: "Why did you change, and give me a false thing, which was taken from me at once? It would have been better for me to have no children at all!" The Baal Shem Tov answered: "I said that you would have children. Surely the child will live." The man waited a long time, and the child did not get up and did not move, and there was no breath of life in him. And he had no face to speak with the Baal Shem Tov again, since he was making him a faithful promise.

He came to the Baal Shem Tov, and spoke with him from another side: he asked him whether to prepare for the circumcision, and whom to honor. The Baal Shem Tov told him to prepare as is the custom, and ordered that a certain man be honored with being sandek, and so too with the cutting, and for himself he took the *metzitzah*. And so they did. They brought the child to the synagogue, and circumcised him, and no blood came out, as when one cuts dead flesh. The Baal Shem Tov began to say the blessings after the circumcision. When he said the words "Sustain this child for his father and his mother," he lingered a long time, until the child's soul came back into him, and blood spurted from the circumcision up to the beams of the ceiling. And with God's help the child lived fully.

**Summary:** The Baal Shem Tov promised a childless man a son, and the son was born in his mother's old age, but died within a few days. The angry father came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov said that the child would surely live, and ordered the circumcision prepared as usual. At the circumcision no blood came out of the dead baby, but when the Baal Shem Tov said "Sustain this child for his father and his mother" and lingered a long time, the soul came back into the baby, the blood spurted up to the ceiling, and the child lived.

## 205 — The Doctor and the Pulse

Once a great and famous doctor came to the duchess of the town. The duchess boasted in praise of the Baal Shem Tov, that he was a great man, and also expert in medicine. The doctor said: "Send for him to come here." She said: "That is not in keeping with his honor. One must send a fine carriage for him, as for noblemen, for he is a great man." She sent for him, and he came before them. The doctor asked the Baal Shem Tov: "Is it true that you are expert in medicine?" He answered him: "It is true." He said: "In what place, and from what expert did you learn?" He said: "God taught me." And the thing was a joke in the doctor's eyes. He asked him further: "Do you understand the pulse?" The Baal Shem Tov said: "Look, I have a certain defect. You understand my pulse, and I will look at and examine your pulse." The doctor took the Baal Shem Tov's pulse, and understood that there was some defect, but did not know what it was, for truly it was being "lovesick" for God, which is beyond his understanding. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov took hold of the doctor by his pulse, and turned his face to the duchess, and asked her: "Were such-and-such precious things stolen from you?" She said: "Yes. They were stolen several years ago, and I do not know where they are." The Baal Shem Tov said: "Send to his lodging, and open his chest, and you will find all the things whole." She sent at once, and found it as his holy words said. And the doctor left there in disgrace and shame.

(And the explanation according to the Kabbalah: the word "*dofek*" (pulse) in gematria is 184, the same number by which the "back of Abba" is called, and so the tzaddikim recognized all the sins in the pulse, as is written in the holy Zohar and in the *Tikkunei HaZohar*.)

**Summary:** A famous doctor who came to the duchess mocked the Baal Shem Tov, who said that God had taught him medicine, and tested him by the pulse. The doctor felt in the Baal Shem Tov's pulse a defect and did not know what it was, for it was the love of God. The Baal Shem Tov, when he felt the doctor's pulse, revealed that he was the one who had stolen the duchess's precious things years before, and they were found in his chest, and the doctor left there in shame.

## 206 — The Calf's Head

Once, on a journey of the Baal Shem Tov, he stayed with the rabbi of a community, to spend the Sabbath there. The rabbi said to his wife: "Know how to honor him, for he is a great and famous man." His wife said: "Why, thank God, we bought meat. We will choose every good piece of it in honor of the guest." When she went to look at the meat, she found nothing, and her heart truly broke within her. She went to her neighbor, perhaps she could borrow from her, and the neighbor too found nothing of what she had prepared. She went to a second neighbor too, and they found nothing. She took counsel with her husband what to do. Meanwhile she saw the town's shochet passing in front of the window, and told him the trouble of her heart, everything that had happened to her. He said: "Why, two or three hours ago I slaughtered a good young calf." The Baal Shem Tov answered and said: "I very much like to eat the head. If possible, let him bring it here whole, and devein it (clean it of the forbidden fats and sinews) here." The shochet said: "Good." And he got up, and went quickly, and brought the head to the rabbi's house.

The Baal Shem Tov began to speak with the shochet: "People say there is some way of telling, some difference, by the teeth. Please check, and count how many teeth it has." The shochet opened the animal's mouth, and put his hand into it to count the teeth. And at once his hand was clamped, so that he could not take it out of there, and the mouth squeezed his hand very hard, until he could not bear it, and cried out loudly. The Baal Shem Tov said: "What is the matter with you, that you cry out? There is no breath of life in it!" And the Baal Shem Tov shouted at him: "Wicked man, confess!" And the shochet confessed his sin: that he had never checked any lung, and whatever he wanted he declared fit, and whatever he wanted he declared unfit. And the Baal Shem Tov gave him a penance for it.

Afterward they went to the synagogue. The Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over the rabbi's face, and the rabbi saw several harmful spirits and ghosts sitting on the synagogue, and was greatly astonished at it. The Baal Shem Tov answered: "The cantor became impure with a seminal emission every time before his prayer." And they removed him from his post. <!-- MATURE: a cantor's nocturnal emission -->

**Summary:** At a certain rabbi's house all the meat prepared in honor of the Baal Shem Tov suddenly vanished, at the neighbors' too, and the shochet offered a calf he had slaughtered. The Baal Shem Tov asked for the head, and when the shochet put his hand in to count the teeth, his hand was clamped in the dead mouth until he cried out, and the Baal Shem Tov ordered him to confess, and he admitted that he had never checked lungs. In the synagogue the Baal Shem Tov showed the rabbi harmful spirits sitting on the building, and explained that the cantor was not careful of his purity before the prayer, and they removed him.

## 207 — The Passing of the Baal Shem Tov

I will write a little about his passing, which was on the first day of Shavuot. On the Passover before that Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz was with the Baal Shem Tov, and he was a little weak. And because of this, on the eve of the last festival day of Passover, he went back and forth whether to go to the mikveh or not, and decided not to go. On the seventh day of Passover, during the prayer, he saw that it had been decreed that the Baal Shem Tov would soon pass away, because he had exerted himself greatly against the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi, as is explained above in the story of Yom Kippur, when they wanted to burn the Babylonian Talmud. Rabbi Pinchas began to exert himself in prayer, and it did not help. And he was very sad that he had not gone to the mikveh, for had he seen this thing in the mikveh, in his opinion it would have helped. After the prayer the Baal Shem Tov asked him whether he had gone to the mikveh the day before, and he said: "No." The Baal Shem Tov said: "The deed is already done, and after a deed there is nothing." After Passover the Baal Shem Tov fell ill with diarrhea, and even so he gathered a little strength, and led the prayers before the ark. And to the disciples who were known for exerting themselves in prayer he said nothing, and sent them to other places. And Rabbi Pinchas did not go home.

On the night of Shavuot the people close to him gathered, to stay awake all night, as in the Arizal's "Tikkun of Shavuot night." And he said words of Torah before them on the matter of the order and on the giving of the Torah. In the morning he sent for the people close to him, that they should all gather, and commanded Rabbi Leib Kessler and another man (whose name I have forgotten) to see to his burial. He taught them on his own body: on each limb he showed them signs of how his soul was leaving that limb, and afterward another limb, so that they would understand about the sick, for they were members of the burial society. And he ordered that a minyan come in to pray with him. And he ordered that they give him the prayer book, and said: "I will converse a little more with God." After the prayer Rabbi Nachman of Horodenka went to the study house to pray for him. The Baal Shem Tov said: "He is making a stir for nothing. If he could go in through the gate I used to go in by, he would achieve something with his prayer."

At that time the soul of a dead man came into him, for him to repair it. He rebuked it and said: "For eighty years you have wandered and roamed, and you have not heard until today that I am in the world? Get out, wicked one!" And at once he said to the servant: "Run quickly, with a great shout, that they clear the way, for I have angered him, in case he harms someone." And so it was: he harmed a girl, the daughter of the shammes. When the servant came back, he heard the Baal Shem Tov saying: "I forgive you these two hours. Do not torment me." The servant said to him: "With whom is your honor speaking?" He said: "Do you not see the Angel of Death? He always fled from me, as people say: 'they chase him off to where the black pepper grows.' And now that they have given him permission over me, his shoulders have widened, and there is great joy on him."

Afterward all the people of the town came to greet him on the festival, and he said words of Torah before them. Afterward, at the meal, he ordered the servant to give mead in a big bottle, and the servant gave it in a small one. He said: "'There is no ruling over the day of death': even the attendant does not obey me." Afterward he said: "Until now I have done kindness with you. And now you do kindness with me." Afterward he went to the outhouse, and the servant wanted to go after him. He asked him: "What is different about today from all the days, that you want to go after me? What have you seen in me?" And the servant did not go after him. He also told them the sign: when he passed away, both clocks would stop. When he came back, he washed his hands, and the big clock stopped, and his people surrounded him, so that he would not see that the clock had stopped.

He said to them: "I know that the clock has stopped, and I am not worried for myself, for I know clearly that I will go out of this gate, and at once go in by another gate." He sat on his bed, and ordered them to stand around his bed, and told them words of Torah about the pillar by which one rises from the lower Garden of Eden to the upper Garden of Eden, and so in each and every world, how it is, and explained the order of the service. He ordered them to say "May the pleasantness," and lay down and sat up several times, and concentrated on the mystical intentions, until they could no longer hear the shaping of his letters. He ordered them to cover him with a sheet, and began to shake and tremble, as in the Amidah prayer, and afterward rested little by little, and they saw that the small clock had stopped. They waited a long time, and put a feather on his nose, and saw that he had passed away.

All this I heard from Rabbi Yaakov of Mezhibuzh, who passed away in the Holy Land. And the rabbi said that Rabbi Leib Kessler saw his soul go out like a flame the color of blue. Once, several weeks before his passing, Rabbi Leib came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov told him that he would surely die. And Rabbi Leib said: "A head for a head and eyes for eyes" (that he was ready to die in his place), and the Baal Shem Tov did not want it. And he said: "Let them give me back what they took from me about a year ago." And he did not want to live, for why should he have such a life?

**Summary:** On the last Passover Rabbi Pinchas of Koretz saw in prayer that the Baal Shem Tov had been sentenced to pass away because of his struggle against the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi, and was sorry that he had not immersed, for he thought he could then have canceled the decree. On the night of Shavuot the Baal Shem Tov studied with his disciples, and in the morning he taught the men of the burial society on his own body how the soul leaves each limb, drove out the soul of a wicked man, and spoke with the Angel of Death, who until now had fled from him. He said that when he passed away both clocks would stop, and after the big clock stopped, he said Torah about the rising from world to world, ordered them to say "May the pleasantness," covered himself with a sheet, and passed away as the small clock stopped.

## 208 — His Son, Rabbi Tzvi

I heard: when the Baal Shem Tov's wife died, his people and his household told him to marry another wife. The Baal Shem Tov answered them in astonishment: "I need a wife? I was separated from my wife fourteen years, and this 'Hershel,' my son, was born by the word." On the day of his death his son, Rabbi Tzvi, was asleep, and they said to the Baal Shem Tov: "Why do you not give your son your last instructions?" He said: "What can I do, when he is asleep?" And the son was asleep because he did not believe that his father would die. They woke him, and said to him: "Your father said that he will surely die today." He came to his father, and began to weep.

His father said to him: "I know that I gave you a soul. For at the hour I was joined with my wife, all the heavens trembled. And had I wished, it was in my power to bring down the soul of Adam, the first man, by the secret of *ibbur*, and I would have known everything that needs to be known. But you have a holy soul, and you do not need all this." The son asked him: "Even so, tell me something." And the father began to tell him. The son said: "I do not understand what you are saying." And the father said: "The world is overpowering me, and I cannot speak with you." And he taught him one name, and said: "Concentrate on this name, and you will see me, and I will study with you." The son said to him: "Perhaps I will forget this name?" And the father told him a remedy, so that he would remember the name. But I have forgotten what he told him. <!-- MATURE: marital union and a son born "by the word" -->

**Summary:** When the Baal Shem Tov's wife passed away, he refused to marry another wife, and said that he had been separated from her for fourteen years and his son had been born by the power of the word. On the day of his passing his son Rabbi Tzvi slept, for he did not believe his father would die, and afterward he came and wept. The Baal Shem Tov told him that he had given him a holy soul, tried to teach him and did not manage it, for "the world is overpowering him," and taught him a holy name by which he could see his father and study with him, and a remedy for remembering the name.

## 209 — The Dog and the Fish

One of the disciples of the holy Rabbi Yechiel Michel of Zlotchov once traveled to his master. When he arrived, the rabbi said to him: "Go home at once." And he did as he said. He was truly bewildered, and did not know why and wherefore the rabbi had commanded him to go back home, for he had a great longing to spend the Sabbath with the rabbi. On his way a small dog joined him, pretty and handsome, and the dog pleased him, and he took it onto his wagon, and played with it a great deal all the way. When they reached the ferry on the water, the dog jumped into the river, and began to swim in the river, and could not swim well, and began to drown in the river, and made many movements, and struggled hard, until it drowned. And that man felt very great sorrow over the drowning of the dog, which had pleased him.

Meanwhile, as he waited there, the fishermen brought up a big fish in their net. He was near his home, and it was the Sabbath eve. So he bought the fish from the fishermen, and when he came home, he prepared the fish in all kinds of ways, in honor of the Sabbath. When he came from immersing, he tasted from the fish, as our Sages said: "Those who taste it merit life." And at once he was turned into another man: his face shone, and the whole Sabbath his prayers were like those of a great and holy man, and at every meal he spoke secrets and hidden things of the Torah, which no ear had ever heard. All the people were amazed, and he himself was amazed too. And when he finished the evening prayer and Havdalah, he went back to being as before, and was greatly astonished.

The next day he traveled again to the rabbi, and the rabbi called him "a one-day Torah scholar." He asked him what this was, and what it was for. The rabbi answered him: "Your father had one sin that he committed, and he was reincarnated in a dog. And so, because 'the heart knows its own bitterness,' you loved the dog very much. And by your grieving much over its drowning, it was raised up, and merited to be reincarnated in the fish the fishermen brought up. And by your preparing the fish in honor of the Sabbath, he was given permission to be joined to you (to enter you). So all Sabbath you had wondrous words. And after Havdalah your father went up to the place fitting for him, and you went back to being as before."

**Summary:** Rabbi Michel of Zlotchov sent a disciple home as soon as he arrived, and on the way a small dog that pleased him joined him, and the dog drowned in the river, and the disciple was very sorry about it. Afterward he bought a fish that the fishermen had brought up there, prepared it for the Sabbath, and when he tasted it, he became a holy man who spoke secrets of the Torah all Sabbath, until Havdalah. Rabbi Michel explained to him that his father had been reincarnated in the dog, had risen through his sorrow to a reincarnation in the fish, and through the preparation for the Sabbath had entered him for one Sabbath.

## 210 — The Child Before the Heavenly Court

Once, at the house of the holy Rabbi Michel of Zlotchov, his young son was very, very ill. And just then news reached the rabbi that in a certain country they wanted to burn the holy books of Rabbi Yaakov Yosef of Polonne the rabbi. The rabbi left his home, and commanded his household that if, Heaven forbid, his son died, they should wait with the burial until he came back home. After he left, the child fainted away (what is called in Yiddish *hinerplet*), and they thought the child had died. After three days sweat came over him, and his soul came back into his body. When he had recovered a little, he told them everything that had happened:

As soon as his soul left, an angel took it, and led it to a palace. The angel had no permission to enter the palace; only the child himself went into the palace. He stood by the door, and saw that the great court was sitting. And at once he saw two messengers carrying a book in which all the sins were written, and it was heavy on them like a heavy load. And at once an angel came with the book of merits, and they were not equal, the one against the other. Until they brought a third book, the book of the sufferings he had received, as our Sages said: "How far do sufferings reach," etc., and many of the sins fell away. Even so, because of the sins that remained, they wanted to sentence him to death, and wanted to write a note.

And at once his father, Rabbi Michel the rabbi, came, with a great commotion and a great outcry, because they wanted to burn those books: "It is revealed and known before the Master of the worlds that he did not do it for his own honor, Heaven forbid, but for the honor of God and the honor of His blessed Torah!" When he saw his son standing by the door, he said to him: "My son, what are you doing here?" He answered him: "I have been standing here a long time, and I do not know why or wherefore. I ask you to speak well on my behalf." He said: "If I do not forget, I will speak well of you." And he cried out again about the books, as before. The court answered that this matter did not belong to their court, but to "one higher than the high," etc. And Rabbi Michel the rabbi went away, and did not remember his son.

Meanwhile the holy rabbi Rabbi Yaakov Yosef, the author of the book, went up to that palace, and cried out and wailed three times, with weeping and great complaint, because they wanted to burn holy books. The rabbi saw him, standing by the door, and asked him: "Yosef, what are you doing here?" And the child answered him as before, and asked him to speak on his behalf. And he answered him as before: "If I do not forget, I will speak well of you." And they answered him in the court as before. And meanwhile he too went away.

And the child stood in despair, for he had no helper and no supporter. And at once a sound of great noise was heard, until truly all the worlds shook, and they proclaimed: "Make way, for the Baal Shem Tov himself is coming to the palace!" The Baal Shem Tov saw him standing by the door, and asked him: "Yosef, what is your business here?" And the child answered him as before, "and I also asked my lord father and the Rabbi of Polonne to speak well on my behalf, and they forgot me. So I ask your holy honor to speak well on my behalf." And the Baal Shem Tov asked the court to release the boy in peace, and commanded him to go home. The child wanted to stay there, and see what the Baal Shem Tov would do there. And at once two men came, and took him, and went with him until they found a stinking carcass lying on the rubbish heap. They said to him: "Go into this carcass." And he did not want to in any way. He cried out and pleaded before them, and they beat him so that he would go in against his will. And when he went into the carcass, he began to sweat.

**Summary:** The young son of Rabbi Michel of Zlotchov fainted and was taken for dead for three days, and during that time his soul stood at the door of the heavenly court, which wanted to sentence him to death. His father, and after him Rabbi Yaakov Yosef of Polonne, came to the palace to cry out against the burning of the books of the *Toldot*, and both promised to speak for him and forgot. Finally the Baal Shem Tov himself came, and all the worlds shook, and he asked that the child be released, and two men forced him back into his body, which seemed to him like a carcass, and he began to sweat and woke up.

Finished and complete. Praise to God, Creator of the world.
